# SAND-T2 — the table

Synthetic content: invented characters in an invented campaign (Table C's Beregost files, docs/campaigns/baldurs-gate-en). Stage origin/claude/handoff at 23220d6 (docs/stages/GAME_PARTY_1.md, the amendment of 2026-10-27 16:36 LA); design sha256 `e0013ebdeea07e4b70b6848ff4705c4d7994c44abc7f5ff2c2aa6a36426671c2`.

Arm **P-SAND**, triad T2; 25 round(s) of 25; ended by rounds. The DM: Claude Opus 5.5 with the owner's settings (Opus writes — Opus records, depth high → xhigh); the relay: the same model at medium. Started 2026-10-28T03:58:38.165Z.

Seats: 1. Bram Halloway (fighter) — Claude Fable 5.1; 2. Sefa Marrin (warlock) — GPT-6 Astra; 3. Pell Dunmore (rogue) — DeepSeek V4 Pro 0813. The players are not told what the others are.

Known condition: Seat 1's character (Bram) is the app's hero record; Sefa and Pell are party companions with full records. Known condition, not corrected: the DM's frame is written around the hero and may give that seat more attention; Heroic Inspiration is the hero's only; the allowed rerolls (2 per session) are the table's, shared by all three. The rotation puts each family in that seat once per triad.

**No hidden layer:** P-SAND plays pure simulation (no prep).

The world's snapshot: sha256 `1649b61c08e027ff44de2bb5065046f872e383a00c5d04803d95553f58568f5a` (the same wizard, sheets and opening for both triads).

## The opening

**The DM's opening (as committed by the wizard):**

````text
Beregost smells of coal smoke and hot iron even in here. Through the shutters comes the steady ring of hammers from the smithies along the Coast Way, and the light is going amber. The Burning Wizard's common room is filling up for the evening: carters, a pair of dusty sellswords, townsfolk with soot still on their forearms. The benches are crowded, and that is how three strangers end up at the same table.

Bram Halloway sits with his back to the wall and his shield propped against the bench, the old company mark painted over. Across from him is Sefa Marrin, a young woman just off the road from Baldur's Gate. Beside her is Pell Dunmore, a lean man from the Lower City with a healer's satchel slung at his hip. He came in by the western road and sat down so quietly that nobody heard him do it.

None of them has said a word to the others yet. Then Orla Tarnwick, the innkeeper, pushes through the crowd with a jug in one hand and three cups hooked on her fingers. She sets them down hard and looks from one face to the next.

"Last free seats in the house, and you three took them together," she says. "So: one tab or three? And what brings each of you into Beregost tonight?"
````

## Round 1

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 1 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram nods at the innkeeper and puts a silver on the table in front of his own cup.

"Three tabs. I'll pay my own as I go." He takes the cup, drinks, and sets it down. "Up from Nashkel. Caravan paid off there, so I'm between work. Bed for the night, hot food if you've got it, and a word on whether anyone's hiring guards northbound."

Then he looks across at the two strangers — a plain look, the kind he'd give a new hire on the first morning of a run — and says, "Bram Halloway. Caravan guard. Was, anyway." He tips his head at the woman first, then the quiet man with the satchel. "And you two? You've got the look of the road on you, and he's got the look of a man who knows how to sit down without being noticed. I'd not mind knowing who I'm sharing a bench with."

Sefa Marrin: “Three tabs, please. We haven’t had time to become bad influences on one another.”

I ease my pack beneath the bench, keeping it clear of everyone’s feet.

“Sefa Marrin, down from Baldur’s Gate. Tonight, supper and a bed are ambition enough. What would those cost?”

I turn to include both strangers, my gaze briefly catching on the healer’s satchel.

“I used to work in a hospice. If either of you has been saving a tale about a blister for someone who can bear to hear it, you’ve chosen your table well.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell lets the innkeeper's gaze pass over him a moment before he answers, one hand resting on the table near his cup. "Three," he says easily. "And a cup of whatever's hot."

He tips a silver piece onto the table and meets the two strangers' eyes in turn — not unfriendly, just the calm, weighing look of a man who's used to sitting down beside people he doesn't know. "Pell. Passing through." He leaves it there, plainly more interested in what the other two might say.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla doesn't bother with the coin until all three of them have spoken. Then she takes Bram's silver, drops four coppers' worth of it into her apron and pushes six coppers back across the wood to him.

"Three tabs. Suits me, less arguing at the end." She points the jug at Sefa. "Supper's mutton stew and black bread, three silver, and it's been on since noon, so it's hot. For a bed it's five silver in the loft, straw and blankets, and you share it with a dozen carters who snore. Eight gets you a room with a door that bolts. Pay in the morning or pay now, just don't pay never."

She looks at Pell's silver, then at him. "Hot means spiced cider, there's a pot by the fire. Five coppers." She takes the coin and counts five coppers back next to his cup without hurrying, the way someone does who's done it ten thousand times.

Then she turns back to Bram, and for the first time she looks him over properly: the mended shoulder on his mail, the painted-over shield. "Up from Nashkel, and you've still got your wagon's worth of teeth. That's news here these days. Southbound, nobody's hiring guards, they want twice as many and can't find them." She jerks her chin toward the hearth. A heavy man in a good brown coat sits alone at the end of the long table there, with a ledger open beside his bowl. "Northbound, there's Gerrit Vosk. He's a cloth factor, out of Amn by way of Nashkel. He's taking three wagons up to the Gate and he's been short of men since his last pair quit him here two days ago. He didn't tell me why they quit. I didn't ask, and they didn't drink enough to tell me."

Someone across the room shouts for her by name. Orla sets the jug in the middle of the table.

"Stew, then. Three bowls, unless one of you says otherwise." She's already turning away. "I'll bring the cider."
````

## Round 2

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 2 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram scoops the coppers off the table without counting them twice and nods at the innkeeper's back. "Stew for me. Loft's fine. I've slept through worse than carters."

He turns his cup a quarter-turn on the wood and looks across at the man by the hearth without making a show of it — the brown coat, the ledger, the way he sits alone with his back to the room.

"Two men quit him here, two days ago, and nobody's saying why." He says it half to himself and half to the table. "On this road, in this year, a guard who walks away from a paid run north has a reason. Could be the pay. Could be the man. Could be what's in the wagons." He drinks. "I've been paid off before I knew what I was carrying exactly once. Didn't care for how that run ended."

To Sefa he gives a short nod. "Hospice work. That's harder than mine, most days. I've a healer's kit I've used more than I'd like, and I'd not say no to someone who knows which end of it to open first." To Pell, with the same plain look: "Passing through is fair enough. Most of us are."

He pushes his shield a little further under the bench so it's out of the walkway, and settles back. "I'll eat first. Then I'll go and have a word with Master Vosk, and I'll ask him straight what happened to his last two. If the answer's good, I'll likely take the work. If either of you is heading north anyway and wants company on the road, say so now — a factor short of men doesn't look too hard at who's walking beside the wagons, and three strangers who've shared a bowl are better than three who haven't."

Sefa Marrin: “Stew for me, yes. And the room with a bolt.” I count out eleven silver and set them where she can collect them.

At Bram’s question, I add, “A healer, mostly. I can help defend a wagon, though you wouldn’t want me holding the shield.”

I glance toward the cloth factor, then back to Bram. “I’ve just come down that road. Not eager to turn straight around, but I’d listen to an offer.”

I lower my voice enough that it stays at our table. “Two guards quitting together might mean trouble on the road—or trouble getting paid. Worth asking which before promising him anything.”

I leave Pell’s short introduction alone and shift the jug within everyone’s reach. “How was the road up from Nashkel?”

Pell Dunmore: Pell picks up the five coppers and pockets them with the ease of a man who counts small change without looking at it. He leans back a little, not far enough to be careless, and takes in the room over the rim of his cup.

"Pell Dunmore," he says to Bram, as if that's the whole of it. "You like knowing who's on your bench. Now you know as much as I'd tell a friend of an hour."

He turns the cup once on the scarred wood, then looks at Sefa. "Hospice work, and now the road. That's a long step from clean linen to dust." It's not a challenge — more like he's trying to see if she'll put a name to what made her leave. He doesn't glance toward Vosk again, but he noticed him: the good coat, the ledger, the two men short. Let Bram run his own errand there. If the factor's looking for blades and a healer, Pell can listen a while longer before he spends his own name on the man.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla comes back through the crowd with a tray on her hip: three bowls of mutton stew with a slab of black bread across each, and a clay mug of cider that steams and smells of clove. She sets the bowls down one at a time and puts the mug in front of Pell.

She sweeps Sefa's eleven silver into her apron and counts it there with her thumb, unhurried. "Stew and the room. Top of the stairs, second door on the left. The bolt's on the inside and it works. That's more than I can say for the one across the hall." She turns to Bram. "Loft and stew for you makes eight silver. Coin now or on the tab, your choice." To Pell: "Your stew's on yours. Three silver."

Someone at the hearth calls for more bread and she's gone again.

The stew is thick and salty, and the mutton has been cooking long enough to fall apart on the spoon. While the table eats, the man at the hearth end of the long table goes on with his business. He spoons stew with his right hand and keeps his left spread flat on the open ledger, as if the room's draught might take the page. Bram is watching him without seeming to, and he catches one thing more. The inn's back door opens onto the yard, where the wagons stand, and every time it bangs, Gerrit Vosk looks up at it. He doesn't look toward the front door at all. He has done it twice since the bowls came. The second time a stable boy comes in stamping mud off his feet, and Vosk watches him all the way to the kitchen before he goes back to his columns.

Earlier, when Orla pointed the jug in his direction, he must have seen it. Once he glances down the long table to theirs, at the shield half under the bench and the mended mail. He holds the look just long enough for it to be a look, then dips his head a fraction, the way a man does across a market when he isn't buying yet. He turns a page.

At the table, Sefa's question to Bram about the Nashkel road is still waiting for an answer, and Pell has made his remark about clean linen and dust.

 1) Bram's stew and loft bed are ordered and his stew has arrived. I didn't take payment because this round he didn't hand over any coin, so Orla asked "now or on the tab." If his earlier "I'll pay my own as I go" stands, tell me and the eight silver goes across.

2) Bram said he'd eat first and then talk to Vosk. The bowls have only just come and the table's questions to him are still open, so that conversation is his to start next round. No roll is needed to walk over.

3) The offer of the healer's kit was spoken aloud, and the shield went under the bench as he described. Neither needs a roll, and how Sefa and Pell answer is up to their players.

4) Sefa's order is done: Orla took her eleven silver, and the stew and the room with the bolt are paid for.
````

````text
She sweeps your eleven silver into her apron and counts it there with her thumb, unhurried. "Stew and the room. Top of the stairs, second door on the left. The bolt's on the inside and it works. That's more than I can say for the one across the hall." She turns to Bram. "Loft and stew for you makes eight silver. Coin now or on the tab, your choice." To Pell: "Your stew's on yours. Three silver."

Someone at the hearth calls for more bread and she's gone again.

The stew is thick and salty, and the mutton has been cooking long enough to fall apart on the spoon. While you eat, the man at the hearth end of the long table goes on with his business. He spoons stew with his right hand and keeps his left spread flat on the open ledger, as if the room's draught might take the page. Once he glances down the long table to yours, at the shield half under the bench and the mended mail. He holds the look just long enough for it to be a look, then dips his head a fraction, the way a man does across a market when he isn't buying yet. He turns a page.

Your question to Bram about the Nashkel road is still waiting for an answer.
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram scoops his coppers off the table and nods at the innkeeper's back. "Stew for me. Loft's fine. I've slept through worse than carters." He turns his cup a quarter-turn on the wood and looks across at the man by the hearth without making a show of it. "Two men quit him here, two days ago, and nobody's saying why," he says, half to himself and half to the table. "On this road, in this year, a guard who walks away from a paid run north has a reason. Could be the pay. Could be the man. Could be what's in the wagons." He drinks. "I've been paid off before I knew what I was carrying exactly once. Didn't care for how that run ended."

He gives Sefa a short nod. "Hospice work. That's harder than mine, most days. I've a healer's kit I've used more than I'd like, and I'd not say no to someone who knows which end of it to open first." To you, with the same plain look: "Passing through is fair enough. Most of us are." He pushes his shield a little further under the bench, out of the walkway, and settles back. "I'll eat first. Then I'll go and have a word with Master Vosk, and I'll ask him straight what happened to his last two. If the answer's good, I'll likely take the work. If either of you is heading north anyway and wants company on the road, say so now. A factor short of men doesn't look too hard at who's walking beside the wagons, and three strangers who've shared a bowl are better than three who haven't."

Sefa orders stew and the room with a bolt, and counts out eleven silver where the innkeeper can collect it. To Bram she says, "A healer, mostly. I can help defend a wagon, though you wouldn't want me holding the shield." She glances toward the cloth factor, then back to Bram. "I've just come down that road. Not eager to turn straight around, but I'd listen to an offer." She lowers her voice so it stays at your table. "Two guards quitting together might mean trouble on the road, or trouble getting paid. Worth asking which before promising him anything." She leaves your introduction alone and shifts the jug within everyone's reach. "How was the road up from Nashkel?"

Orla comes back through the crowd with a tray on her hip: three bowls of mutton stew with a slab of black bread across each, and a clay mug of cider that steams and smells of clove. She sets the bowls down one at a time and puts the mug in front of you.

She sweeps Sefa's eleven silver into her apron and counts it there with her thumb, unhurried. "Stew and the room. Top of the stairs, second door on the left. The bolt's on the inside and it works. He spoons stew with his right hand and keeps his left spread flat on the open ledger, as if the room's draught might take the page. Once he glances down the long table to yours, at the shield half under the bench and the mended mail. He holds the look just long enough for it to be a look, then dips his head a fraction, the way a man does across a market when he isn't buying yet. He turns a page.
````

## Round 3

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 3 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram counts eight silver out onto the wood for Orla's next pass and goes back to his bowl.

"Quiet, if you want the honest answer," he says to Sefa between spoonfuls. "Too quiet. Four days up from Nashkel and I passed one tinker and a militia patrol out of here that didn't want to talk. No caravans southbound at all, and the ruts on the road were old. The Friendly Arm way, people are saying wagons go missing between here and Nashkel, guards and all — not robbed and left, missing. I didn't see sign of it, but I kept to daylight and slept cold, no fire." He tears bread and wipes the bowl. "That's why he can't get men. Nobody wants to walk that stretch, and anyone who's already walked it once isn't in a hurry to go back."

He glances down the table once more, not at Vosk's face but at his hand on the ledger, and then at the back door when it bangs.

"He's not watching for customers," he says quietly to the pair of them. "Third time now he's looked at the yard and not once at the street. Man's worried about his wagons, not his trade." To Pell, with a shrug: "Friend of an hour is more than most get. I'll take it."

He finishes the stew, wipes his mouth on the back of his hand, and stands, leaving the shield where it is under the bench. "Keep an eye on that for me. I'll go and ask him."

He walks down the long table to the hearth end and stops across from Gerrit Vosk — not looming, just standing where the man has to look up from his columns.

"Master Vosk. Bram Halloway. Orla says you're short two guards for the run north and I'm between work." He lets that sit a breath. "Before we talk pay, I'd like to know why your last two quit you here. And why you're watching your yard door instead of your soup."

Sefa Marrin: I break off a piece of bread, considering Pell’s remark.

“The linen wasn’t always clean. But yes, it’s a change.” I give him a small smile. “I wanted to find out what else I could do. So far, mostly walk.”

To Bram, I nod. “I’ll come and hear him out with you after supper. No promise beyond that yet.”

I take a few spoonfuls while the stew is hot, then ask Bram, “You said you didn’t care for how that run ended. Did you find out what you were carrying?”

Pell Dunmore: Pell eats a few spoonfuls, unhurried, then answers Sefa with a tilt of his head. "Dust pays better than hospice, if you don't mind the occasional arrow."

He turns the spoon in the bowl, then adds, "The west road was quiet. A broken cart, a dead snake, nothing worth telling." A pause, then to Bram: "If you mean to put questions to the factor, I can sit near enough to hear. Not to take work yet — but to know who's hiring, and why." He glances toward the man at the hearth for just a moment, then back. "He noticed the shield and the mended mail. He's not buying yet, but he's looking."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla comes past with an empty tray and a full jug. She sweeps Bram's eight silver into her apron without breaking stride. "Loft and stew. Paid." Then she's gone toward the kitchen.

The back door bangs again while Bram is talking. A carter comes in with a sack of oats on his shoulder, and down at the hearth end the brown coat turns toward the yard once more, and turns back.

Sefa has her answer for Pell ready before Bram is on his feet, and her question for Bram is out while he's still rising. "Did you find out what you were carrying?" It reaches him as he steps clear of the bench, with his shield left behind. Pell tells Sefa that dust pays better if you don't mind the odd arrow. The west road gets a broken cart and a dead snake from him. When he makes his offer to sit close enough to hear, Bram is already three steps down the long table. Pell says it to Sefa as much as to him. She can tell, though, that his eyes went to the hearth for a moment and back.

Bram stops across the table from Gerrit Vosk. Vosk finishes the spoonful he's on. He closes the ledger over one finger to keep the place, and only then looks up. Close to, he's older than the coat suggests: grey in the beard, a bad night's sleep under each eye, and a ring on his little finger worn down nearly smooth.

He hears Bram out without interrupting, the first question and then the second. When he answers, he keeps his voice low, under the noise of the room.

"Master Halloway." He says the name carefully, the way a man repeats a figure so he'll remember it. "They wanted more than we had agreed in Nashkel. On this road, this season, every guard south of the Gate thinks he's worth double. I declined, and they declined to go on."

Bram has heard that answer before, from men who meant it, and this isn't how they say it. It comes out too smooth and too ready, like a line Vosk has gone over on the road. He doesn't look at Bram while he says it, either. He looks at the finger keeping his place in the ledger.

Vosk seems to know it landed badly. He breathes out through his nose and sets his spoon down in the bowl.

"And the door." His mouth goes tight. "There was also a disagreement about the cargo. One of them took it into his head that what I carry was his business to look at. It is not. A guard's business is the outside of a wagon." He glances at the yard door, and this time he doesn't bother hiding it. "I have three wagons of Amnian wool and linen standing in a yard I don't own, in a town where two men know exactly what's in them and are no longer in my pay. So yes. I watch the door."

He opens the ledger again, flat under his left hand.

"Two gold a day and your food, Master Halloway. Four days to the Gate if the weather holds, and we leave at first light. Half when we start, half at the Basilisk Gate." His eyes go past Bram's shoulder, down the long table to the woman with the prayer beads and the quiet man with the satchel. Then they come back. "Those two. Are they with you?"

Back at the table, Sefa and Pell heard all of Bram's questions; his voice carries. Vosk's answers came to them only in pieces over the room: *more than we had agreed*, and later *two gold*. The rest stayed down at the hearth.
````

## Round 4

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 4 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't sit. He rests one hand on the table edge, well clear of the ledger, and keeps his voice down to match Vosk's.

"Two gold a day's fair. I'll not haggle it." He lets that settle so the man knows the next part isn't about money. "And you're right that the inside of a wagon's not my business. I've walked ten years on this road not knowing what was under the canvas and never once lifted it. But I'll tell you what I told the last factor who gave me that answer that smooth: I don't need to know what it is. I need to know what kind of trouble it draws. Thieves come for wool one way. They come for other things another way, and in different numbers, and I stand in a different place depending. If I'm standing in the wrong place when it comes, that's your three wagons gone and me dead in the ruts, and neither of us gets our second half."

He glances at the yard door himself, then back.

"So. Two men who've seen your cargo, who know this yard and know your road, and who walked out without their pay. Do they know you leave at first light? Did they leave town, or are they drinking somewhere on this street?" A beat. "And has anyone been near those wagons tonight that you didn't send?"

He tips his head back toward the far table without taking his eyes off Vosk.

"As for those two — I met them over a bowl an hour ago. The woman's a healer, says she's walked down from the Gate. The man says he's passing through. They're not mine to speak for. If they want the work they'll come and say so, and you can weigh them same as you're weighing me." A short, dry breath that isn't quite a laugh. "You're not buying yet. I saw that. Neither am I. Answer me straight on the two men and the wagons and I'll give you a yes before the cider's cold."

Sefa Marrin: “I’d rather hear it from him than collect every third word,” I say to Pell.

I take my pack and lift Bram’s shield by its carrying strap, then walk over to the hearth end of the table. I offer the shield back to Bram rather than leaving it unattended.

“Sefa Marrin. I was considering the road north as well. May I join you?”

I wait for Vosk’s acknowledgment before taking a seat. “I can tend wounds and help defend the wagons. Bram’s questions matter to me, too—what’s changed since you hired the last pair?”

Pell Dunmore: Pell drains the last of his cider and sets the mug down softly. "Watch my bowl," he says to Sefa, low. "I'll be by the fire — nothing to see." Then he rises and walks toward the hearth with an unhurried, aimless amble, turning a little toward the warmth as if that's all he came for.

He stops close enough to the long table to hear what a man nearby might hear, places one boot on the edge of the raised hearth, and lets his attention slide sideways toward Vosk and Bram without looking directly at them.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Vosk hears Bram out the same way as before, without interrupting. His eyes go once to the hand resting on the table edge, well away from his ledger, and he seems to note it.

Before he can answer, Sefa comes down the long table with her pack on one shoulder and Bram's shield hanging from her other hand by its strap. Behind her, Pell's "Watch my bowl" catches up with her just as she rises, too late to do anything about. His bowl and her half-eaten bread stay where they were on the far table.

She holds the shield out to Bram and turns to the factor. "Sefa Marrin. I was considering the road north as well. May I join you?"

Vosk looks at her for a moment: the prayer beads, the road dust on her hem. Then he moves his bowl a hand's width aside and opens his palm toward the bench across from him. It's a factor's gesture, the kind that seats a buyer. "Mistress Marrin."

She sits. A little way off, Pell has drifted over to the fire with his empty mug. He puts one boot up on the raised hearthstone and holds his hands out to the warmth like any man who has been on the road since noon. Vosk's eyes pass over him once, over the satchel at his hip, and then move on. In this crowd a man warming himself by the fire is nothing to look at twice, and Vosk doesn't. From where Pell stands, every low word at the table carries.

"I can tend wounds and help defend the wagons," Sefa says. "Bram's questions matter to me too. What's changed since you hired the last pair?"

"The last pair are gone, Mistress. Apart from that, nothing." Vosk turns back to Bram, and this time he doesn't look down at the ledger. "I will answer you in order, Master Halloway, since you asked in order."

"Do they know when I leave? Yes. In Nashkel I told them two nights' rest in Beregost and away on the third morning. That is tomorrow. I have not changed it, because I cannot afford to sit here another week paying for a yard."

"Did they leave town? One did. Tamsk, the older one, took the south road the morning after we quarrelled. I watched him go from that window." He nods toward the shutters on the street side. "The other is Harn, the one who put his hands where they did not belong. I have not seen him leave. Neither have I gone looking for him."

He stops. When he goes on he speaks more slowly, picking each word, like a man paying out a sum he would rather keep.

"You asked what kind of trouble it draws. That is a fair question, and you asked it fairly. Wool and linen, three wagons, as I said. And among it there is one small crate that is not mine. I carry it on commission, sealed, for a buyer in the Gate. I have not opened it. I will not open it, and no one who walks beside my wagons will open it either." His mouth goes tight. "What thieves it draws, I cannot tell you, because I do not know what it is. That is the truth. I would rather you had it now than learned it on the road."

Then, without waiting to be asked again, he half turns on the bench and lifts two fingers toward the kitchen passage. The stable boy who came in stamping mud a little while ago is leaning there with a heel of bread. He straightens up and comes over, wary of being in trouble.

"Has anyone been at my wagons tonight, Pim?"

The boy shifts his weight. "Nobody touched 'em, master. But..." He glances at Bram's mail and then back at Vosk. "Your other man come by the yard. The tall one, with the red neckcloth. Before the lamps, it was. He asked was the Amnian still going in the morning, and I said I thought so, first light. He give me a copper for it." He looks at the floor. "He didn't go near the wagons. Just stood at the gate and looked a while, and went off up the street."

"Up the street," Vosk says. "Toward the Red Sheaf."

"Might be, master. That way, anyhow."

Vosk lets the boy go with a nod. He sits very still for a moment with his hand flat on the closed ledger. When he looks up at Bram again, the tiredness under his eyes is plainer than before.

"So now you know what I know, Master Halloway. Two gold a day and your food, half at the start and half at the Basilisk Gate. The same for Mistress Marrin, if she stands a watch." He doesn't look toward the fire. "And the same for anyone else who means to. Is it yes?"
````

````text
"Do they know when I leave? Yes. In Nashkel I told them two nights' rest in Beregost and away on the third morning. That is tomorrow. I have not changed it, because I cannot afford to sit here another week paying for a yard."

"Did they leave town? One did. Tamsk, the older one, took the south road the morning after we quarrelled. I watched him go from that window." He nods toward the shutters on the street side. "The other is Harn, the one who put his hands where they did not belong. I have not seen him leave. Neither have I gone looking for him."

He stops. When he goes on he speaks more slowly, picking each word, like a man paying out a sum he would rather keep.

"You asked what kind of trouble it draws. That is a fair question, and you asked it fairly. Wool and linen, three wagons, as I said. And among it there is one small crate that is not mine. I carry it on commission, sealed, for a buyer in the Gate. I have not opened it. I will not open it, and no one who walks beside my wagons will open it either." His mouth goes tight. "What thieves it draws, I cannot tell you, because I do not know what it is. That is the truth. I would rather you had it now than learned it on the road."

Then, without waiting to be asked again, he half turns on the bench and lifts two fingers toward the kitchen passage. The stable boy who came in stamping mud a little while ago is leaning there with a heel of bread. He straightens up and comes over, wary of being in trouble.

"Has anyone been at my wagons tonight, Pim?"

The boy shifts his weight. "Nobody touched 'em, master. But..." He glances at your mail and then back at Vosk. "Your other man come by the yard. The tall one, with the red neckcloth. Before the lamps, it was. He asked was the Amnian still going in the morning, and I said I thought so, first light. He give me a copper for it." He looks at the floor. "He didn't go near the wagons. Just stood at the gate and looked a while, and went off up the street."

"Up the street," Vosk says. "Toward the Red Sheaf."

"Might be, master. That way, anyhow."

Vosk lets the boy go with a nod. He sits very still for a moment with his hand flat on the closed ledger. When he looks up at you again, the tiredness under his eyes is plainer than before.

"So now you know what I know, Master Halloway. Two gold a day and your food, half at the start and half at the Basilisk Gate. The same for Mistress Marrin, if she stands a watch." He doesn't look toward the fire. "And the same for anyone else who means to. Is it yes?"
````

## Round 5

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 5 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram takes the shield from Sefa with a nod of thanks and sets it against his leg.

"Yes," he says. "On two conditions that cost you nothing but a bit of straw."

He holds up one finger. "First. Harn knows the yard, knows the hour, and he came back after dark to make sure of it. A man who's walked away from his pay doesn't come back to look at the gate because he misses the horses. So tonight I don't sleep in your loft, Master Vosk. I sleep in the yard, beside your wagons, with my shield on my arm. You can tell Orla to keep my five silver or put it toward the morning's bread; I don't care which. If Harn or anyone else comes over that gate before first light, I'd rather meet them there than find the canvas cut at dawn."

A second finger. "Second. The crate stays sealed, that's your word and I'll keep it. But I want to know which wagon it rides in and where in the load, so I know which wagon I walk beside when the road narrows. You needn't tell me in this room. Show me in the yard."

He glances at Sefa. "You said you'd hear him out. You've heard him. If you're in, you're in for the same coin, and I'd be glad of you — a healer who can also hold a torch in the yard tonight is worth more to me than a third sword." His eyes go briefly to the man at the fire, warming his hands, and come back without comment.

Then, to Vosk again, quieter: "I'll not go looking for Harn at the Red Sheaf. A man drinking is a man not climbing your fence, and I'd sooner he stayed there. But if he comes to the yard, I'll have a word with him, and it'll be a short one." He picks up the shield. "Where are the wagons?"

Sefa Marrin: “Not quite yet, Master Vosk. A sealed commission is ordinary enough. A former guard coming back to ask when it leaves deserves some care.”

I keep my voice low. “Who entrusted the crate to you, and who receives it in the Gate? I lived there; I may know the name.”

“And forgive me—I missed part of your conversation. When you say Harn put his hands where they didn’t belong, was he trying to open the crate? Did he see anything?”

I rest my hands on the table. “I’m not asking you to break the seal. But before I agree to protect it, I need to know whether you have reason to think it could endanger us without anyone attacking the wagons.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't move from the hearth until Vosk has finished laying out his terms. Then he sets his empty mug on the mantel with a small hollow sound, tugs his hood forward against the evening, and walks toward the yard door as if heading out to the privy. He doesn't look back at the table.

Outside, he steps clear of the doorway and then into the deeper shadow beside the stack of firewood, letting his eyes adjust. He studies the street and the mouth of the yard before he takes another step — looking for anyone loitering, and for a tall man with a red neckcloth.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Vosk hears both conditions through, and doesn't argue either of them.

"Sleep where you like, Master Halloway. If Mistress Tarnwick wants something for the yard, I'll pay it." He nods once, the way a man initials a page. "And yes, I'll show you the wagon. Not in here."

Then Sefa says *not quite yet*. He turns to her and waits, his hands flat on the table, until she has finished all her questions.

"The names are not mine to give, Mistress Marrin. The man who put it in my hands in Nashkel trusted me with them, and the buyer will expect the same of me. I do not tell them to anyone. Not to you, and not to the Fist if it asks." He says it without heat. It's plainly a rule he has kept before.

"Harn. Yes, he was trying to open it. At the noon halt on the second day out of Nashkel I came round the tail of the wagon and found him with his knife under the band of the lid. He had not got it up. He saw nothing of the inside."

Behind them the yard door bangs. Vosk's eyes go to it at once. The quiet man from the fire has left his mug on the mantel and is gone through it with his hood up, like a man on his way to the privy. Vosk watches the door swing shut. He says nothing about it yet, but the skin around his mouth has gone tight.

When he looks back at Sefa, he takes a moment before he answers her last question.

"You asked whether it could harm us with nobody attacking. I do not know. I will tell you why I cannot simply say no." He lowers his voice further. "At night it makes a sound. Low, like bees in a wall. You feel it in the wagon boards more than you hear it. And on the last morning before Beregost there was frost on the lid. White frost, on the lid only. The canvas over it was dry, and the night had not been cold." He breathes out. "Nobody has come to harm from it, that I have seen. But that is what my two men heard and saw, and that is why they would not go on. The pay was never the reason."

He stands, tucks the ledger under his arm and takes a candle-lamp from the end of the table.

"The wagons are in the yard, Master Halloway. I'll show you now." He glances at the door once more. "Your man from the fire is already out there."

---

The yard door shuts behind Pell. The noise of the common room drops to a murmur through the planks.

The yard is packed earth and old straw, and it smells of mules. Along the stable wall stand three wagons in a row with their canvas lashed down and their tongues resting on the ground. In the stable behind them something stamps and blows. A single lantern hangs on a hook by the stable door. Its light reaches the first wagon and part of the second. The third stands in the dark. The privy is in the far corner, and the firewood is stacked shoulder-high beside the door. When Pell steps into the shadow of the stack, the lantern light stops a foot short of his boots.

The yard gate is two leaves of board, about as high as a man's head. One leaf stands propped open on a stone. Through the gap he can see a strip of the Coast Way: lamps at the doors, and a smithy down the road banking its forge for the night, a red glow behind its shutters. Two carters walk past arguing about a wheel. Up the street, the Red Sheaf has every window lit, and a burst of singing comes from it and dies away.

Across the road a boy of about fifteen sits on the step of a cooper's shop, eating something out of a cloth. He has a leather apron on and soot on his arms. Twice in the time Pell watches, the boy looks up, once at the Burning Wizard's front door when it opens and once up the street. Then he goes back to his food.

Nobody is at the gate. Nobody loiters in the street or along the yard wall, and nowhere in the lamplight is there a tall man with a red neckcloth.

Behind Pell the latch of the yard door lifts. Candlelight and Vosk's voice come through the gap.

 1) No roll was needed for the yard: Vosk agreed that Bram sleeps out there, and it's only 19:45. The night watch starts when Bram goes out and settles in, and I won't skip ahead to it for him.

2) Vosk answered "Where are the wagons?" by picking up his lamp and offering to show Bram the wagon now. The turn stopped there because walking out to the yard is Bram's move, so the showing comes next round.

3) Bram said he would not go looking for Harn at the Red Sheaf. Having a word with him only comes up if Harn turns up at the yard, and he hasn't yet. Nothing to roll.

4) No roll was needed for Sefa's questions, because Vosk answered all of them in the scene. He refused to give the names of the sender and the buyer. He told her about the hum at night and the frost on the lid, and said he doesn't know whether the crate could hurt anyone.
````

## Round 6

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 6 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram listens to the part about the bees in the wall and the frost, and his face doesn't change much, but he's quiet a moment longer than he was for the rest.

"That's not thieves' trouble," he says. "That's the other kind. And it's the kind I've no skill for — I can stand between a man and a sword, Master Vosk. I can't stand between a wagon and whatever hums in it." He looks at Sefa. "You've prayer beads and hospice years. You'd know better than me whether frost on a lid in a warm night is something to walk away from. I'll hear it if you say it is."

He shoulders the shield and settles the strap.

"But I said yes, and I don't take a yes back inside the same hour. I'll amend it, though. Your sealed crate rides where you put it, and no one of mine touches it. If it does more than hum and frost — if a horse won't go near it, if a man wakes cold beside it, if that lid lifts on its own — then the wagon stops where it stands and you and I talk again, and that talk may end with the crate on the roadside and the rest of your wool going on to the Gate. Half pay for the whole run either way. That's the price of carrying what you won't name."

He steps aside to let Vosk lead with the lamp, and says to the man's back, low, as they cross toward the yard door:

"And the one from the fire. Pell Dunmore, he called himself, and he was listening to every word of this. He's out there ahead of us. Could be the privy." Bram's hand rests on the longsword's pommel, not drawing, just there. "I'd not mind you walking in behind me rather than in front. Hold the lamp up when we clear the door."

Then, over his shoulder to Sefa, if she's coming: "Bring a torch from the hearth. I'd like to see the frost for myself."

Sefa Marrin: I stand, taking my pack.

“His name is Pell. We met this evening; he hasn’t agreed to work for you.”

I keep my voice low. “Thank you for telling me about the frost. That changes my answer. I know something of the arcane, and I’ll look at the crate from outside, with your permission. No opening it, no disturbing the seal. I can’t promise I’ll recognise what’s happening.”

I glance at Bram. “I’ll come into the yard with you. But I haven’t accepted the journey yet.”

Then back to Vosk: “If I find reason to think it’s dangerous, we discuss that before departure. Sealed needn’t mean ignored.”

I follow him out, intending to examine the crate’s visible markings and any frost without touching it, and listen for the sound he described.
````

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Arcana: what the hum and the cold of the sealed crate mean, judged from outside · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Sefa Marrin · Arcana: what the hum and the cold of the sealed crate mean, judged from outside · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Vosk listens to the amendment standing, with the ledger under his arm and the candle-lamp in his hand. When Bram reaches *the crate on the roadside*, his jaw shifts once.

"The stopping, yes. If a horse refuses it, or a man wakes cold, we halt and we talk. Half pay for the whole run if you walk away after that talk. Those terms I grant you, Master Halloway." He pauses. "I will tell you now that I will argue in that talk. I would rather you knew it here than found it out on the road."

To Sefa he only nods. When she says *sealed needn't mean ignored*, he takes a long moment before he answers. "Look, then. From outside. I would be glad of someone who can tell me what it is I should be afraid of."

He lets Bram go first through the yard door, as Bram asked, and comes out a step behind him with the lamp held high. The noise of the common room drops away behind them. Out here it's mules, old straw and the cold coming down. The single lantern by the stable door lights the first wagon and part of the second, and the woodstack beside the door throws a long shadow. Through the propped gate the Coast Way shows a strip of lamplit doorways, and singing carries down from the Red Sheaf.

Vosk walks past the first wagon to the middle one and climbs onto the driver's bench, grunting with the effort. He unknots two lashings at the front of the canvas and folds it back. Bolts of undyed linen are stacked to the hoops, and wedged in hard behind the bench, where a man on the seat could reach back and lay a hand on it, sits the crate.

It's smaller than either of them would have pictured from the talk: a little over two feet long and a foot and a half deep, dark oak with two iron bands. Under one band there's a fresh pale gouge in the wood where a knife went in and was pulled out again. Over the seam of the lid sits a seal of dark red wax the size of a palm, stamped with a device: a hand closed over a coin, inside a ring of small letters.

Sefa can read the letters as soon as the lamplight reaches them. The script is Amnian, but the words come to her as plainly as her own hand: *SEIZED UNDER THE LAW OF LICENCE. BY ORDER OF THE SIX. TO BE OPENED BY THE COWLED ALONE.*

There's a second mark below the seal, on the end board of the crate, low down. It's no letter at all, so the meaning doesn't come to her the way the words did. A small sigil was burned into the oak, a circle cut by three short strokes that don't meet in the middle. Someone has scraped at it with a blade, and more than half of it is gone.

There's no frost tonight. The lid is dry and so is the canvas. But when Sefa leans in close over the bench, without touching anything, she hears it under the mules and the distant singing: a low, steady hum, like bees behind plaster. Bram, standing at the wheel with his hand on the side-board, feels it come up through the wood into his palm. He also sees that the linen bolt pressed against the crate's far side is dark along its edge. It's damp, as if someone had laid a wet cloth there and taken it away.

Sefa tries to work out what kind of magic makes a sound like that and draws the warmth out of the wood around it.

Sefa listens a long while and gets no further than what anyone with ears could tell. It's magic, it's working now, and it's steady. She can't tell whether the hum is something holding a thing in, or the thing inside doing whatever it does, and the cold fits either one equally well. Nothing in the hospice or in her own reading matches it. The burned sigil means nothing to her either.

Vosk watches her face over the lamp rather than the crate. "Nothing," he says. It isn't quite a question.

He lets the canvas fall back over the linen and starts tying the lashings again with thick, careful fingers. "Middle wagon, behind the bench, Master Halloway. That's where it rides, and that's where I sit by day." He climbs down heavily and doesn't look back at the wagon. "Mistress Tarnwick will have something to say about you sleeping in her yard. I'll go and say it to her first."

He stands holding the lamp and waits to see whether either of them has anything more to say before he goes in.

*(Out of game: Pell is in his own scene this round, so whether Bram and Sefa meet him out here gets settled there.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 6 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays where he is, back to the woodstack, and lets the yard door open without turning. The lantern light will find Vosk and the others before it finds him. He watches the gate and the street past it, and listens for how Vosk answers the question about the crate — which wagon, and where in the load.
````

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Stealth: stay unseen in the woodstack's shadow while Vosk's lamp goes by · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Stealth: stay unseen in the woodstack's shadow while Vosk's lamp goes by · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The latch lifts behind Pell and the door swings out toward the woodstack. Light spills across the packed earth, Vosk's candle-lamp held high, and footsteps go past less than an arm's length from Pell's shoulder. He doesn't turn. He stays where the stack's shadow is deepest and keeps still.

The lamp goes past him. Vosk carries it at head height and looks ahead, the way a man does when he's been told someone is out here and expects to find him in the open. His eyes go first to the propped gate, then to the dark third wagon. They never drop to the shadow at the foot of the stack, and Pell stays where he is.

From the woodstack Pell has a clear view down the row. Vosk walks past the first wagon to the middle one and climbs onto the driver's bench, slowly. He sets the lamp on the seat, unknots two lashings at the front of the canvas and folds it back. The light falls on undyed linen stacked to the hoops. Wedged in tight behind the bench, low enough that a man on the seat could reach back and rest a hand on it, is something squarer and darker than cloth. At this distance Pell can make out a small box of dark wood with iron bands that catch the light, and a smudge of red wax on the lid. He's too far off to see anything finer.

Vosk's voice is low, but the yard is quiet apart from the mules, and it carries to the stack: "Middle wagon, behind the bench, Master Halloway. That's where it rides, and that's where I sit by day."

There's something else, faint enough that Pell isn't sure of it at first. Under the stamping in the stable and the singing up the street, the boards of the woodstack against his back carry a low, steady thrum. It isn't coming from the stack, and he can't hear it any closer than that.

Out in the street, life goes on. A girl in a grey shawl comes down the Coast Way from the direction of the Red Sheaf with a covered basket on her hip. The cooper's boy across the road is on his feet before she reaches him. He wipes his hands on his apron twice, takes the basket from her, and the two of them go round the side of the cooper's shop toward the back, close together, talking. Nobody else comes past the gate. There's still no red neckcloth anywhere in the lamplight.

At the wagon, Vosk lets the canvas fall and starts retying the lashings. He takes his time with them, and he doesn't look back at the crate.
````

## Round 7

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 7 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram keeps his palm on the side-board a moment longer, feeling the hum come up through the oak, then takes his hand away and wipes it on his thigh like a man who's touched something that's been in the sun too long.

"Not nothing," he says to Vosk, not unkindly. "You feel it through the wheel. Your mules feel it through the shafts, I'd wager, which is why your boy says nobody touches them." He points with his chin, not his finger, at the bolt of linen pressed against the crate's far side. "And that's wet. Linen doesn't sweat on its own. Something's coming off that box that leaves water on cloth, and last night it left frost. That's the same thing happening, just colder." He glances at Sefa. "You'd know the word for it. I don't."

He crouches a little to look at the burned sigil on the end board, the circle cut by three strokes with half of it scraped away. "Someone wanted that gone and didn't finish the job. Harn's knife went in under the lid, you said. Was it Harn scraped this too, or was it like that when it came to you in Nashkel?"

Then he straightens and looks round the yard properly — the woodstack and its shadow, the propped gate, the dark beyond the second lantern where the third wagon stands — looking for Pell.

"He came out this door ahead of us and he's not at the privy, or he's the slowest man in Beregost." He says it level, pitched for Vosk and Sefa and anyone standing quiet in the dark. "Dunmore. If you're in this yard, step into the light. I'd sooner see you than guess at you."

To Vosk, before the man goes in: "Have your boy shut that gate and drop the bar. I'll be between the middle wagon and the stable wall, where I can see both. If Mistress Tarnwick wants coin for it, she can have it. And tell her I'll want a lamp of my own left by the door, lit."

Sefa Marrin: “Nothing I can identify,” I correct gently. “The magic is active, but I can’t tell whether it’s containing a danger or causing one. My ignorance isn’t reassurance.”

I step back from the bench and lower my voice to reach Vosk and Bram, not the street.

“I can read the seal. It says: ‘Seized under the Law of Licence. By order of the Six. To be opened by the Cowled alone.’ That points to Amn’s Cowled Wizards. There’s also a burned mark someone has partly scraped away. I don’t recognise it.”

I look at Vosk. “Did you know what the seal said? Have you papers authorising you to carry this out of Amn and deliver it? You needn’t tell me your buyer’s name to answer that.”

Then to Bram: “You were right not to open it. We should leave it untouched.”

I turn back to the factor. “I won’t sign on for the Gate tonight. I’d first want advice from someone better versed in this magic. Would you permit us to seek that advice here, before it goes back onto the road?”
````

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Perception: finding the man who came out ahead of them, somewhere in the yard's dark · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Bram Halloway · Perception: finding the man who came out ahead of them, somewhere in the yard's dark · decide · presses: [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram wipes his palm down his thigh. Vosk watches him do it and says nothing.

When Bram crouches at the end board, the candle-lamp Vosk left on the bench lights the sigil from above: a burned circle cut by three short strokes that don't meet, most of it scraped away. The scraping isn't new. Bram grew up in a smithy yard and knows what a fresh tool mark looks like. Harn's gouge under the band is pale, raw wood. These scrape lines are grey, with road dirt worked into them and the edges gone soft from handling. Someone took a blade to this mark a long time before the crate reached Beregost.

"It was like that in Nashkel," Vosk says from the bench. "Harn's knife went under the band. Nothing else."

Bram straightens and turns to the yard. The lantern by the stable door lights the first wagon and stops short. The third wagon is a dark shape against the wall. The propped gate shows a strip of lamplit road. Beside the door they came out by, the woodstack throws a long shadow that the lantern doesn't reach. He looks into that shadow last and longest.

Bram has just been staring into candlelight, and his eyes won't give the dark back to him. He sees the wagons, the privy door and a stack of split wood. If anyone is standing in that shadow, he can't pick them out.

"Dunmore. If you're in this yard, step into the light. I'd sooner see you than guess at you."

His voice carries flat across the packed earth. In the stable a mule shifts and blows. Up the street the Red Sheaf starts another song.

Vosk heard Bram's orders and doesn't argue with any of them. "The gate barred. A lamp by the door, lit. I'll see to both and square it with Mistress Tarnwick." He turns to Sefa, and while she reads the seal aloud he stands very still. She gets to *the Cowled alone*. His face doesn't change. He has heard those words before.

"Yes, Mistress Marrin. I read Amnian. I have read that seal more times than I would like." He takes a breath. "You ask about papers. I have one paper. It's a bill of release from the seizure house at Nashkel, stamped, and it says the crate was sold out of the forfeit stores with its seal unbroken, to be broken only by the buyer's own licensed hand. The man who gave me the commission told me that is done. Confiscated goods are sold, and the money goes to the Six." He pauses. "It carries the names. You will not see it."

He takes the lamp down off the bench. "As for advice. I leave at first light. I won't sit here another day, and the crate does not go anywhere to be looked at. It stays in this wagon." He looks at the yard gate, then back at her. "But if you can bring someone here tonight, someone who knows more than you do and will keep their mouth shut, they may look at it from outside the way you did, with me standing by. Tonight. Not in the morning." He waits a moment. "Who did you have in mind? A priest at the Morninglord's house would ask me questions in front of half the town."

He stands with the lamp lit and his weight toward the inn door, waiting for her answer before he goes in.

*(Out of game: if Pell answers Bram's call, that happens in his own scene.)*
````

````text
Pell Dunmore: Pell remains still against the woodstack, letting the lamp and the talk move without him. He keeps part of his attention on the street and the gate, but his real listening is closer: the two men at the wagon, the sound of the lashings being retied, and the measured way Vosk moves around that middle wagon.

He notes the crate's place behind the driver's bench and says nothing. Through the stack at his back that odd low thrum is still there, too steady to be the mules. He turns the knowledge over once and files it away.

When Vosk finishes and steps down, Pell eases a little deeper around the corner of the stack, keeping out of the lamp's sweep, and waits to hear what Bram plans for the night — and whether Sefa means to stand the yard with him.
````

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Perception: catching Sefa's lowered voice and Vosk's low answers across the yard, over the mules and the singing · roll · presses: [Roll the die]

**Card** · Pell Dunmore · Perception: catching Sefa's lowered voice and Vosk's low answers across the yard, over the mules and the singing · decide · presses: prose, [Onward]

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell keeps his back to the split wood and lets the yard go on around him.

At the middle wagon, Bram's voice carries without trying. Pell hears *not nothing*, then the part about the mules feeling it through the shafts, and *that's wet. Linen doesn't sweat on its own.* Then Bram crouches out of sight behind the wheel and asks something about a mark and Harn's knife. The answer comes from the bench in Vosk's low, even voice: *It was like that in Nashkel.* After that Sefa speaks, and she has dropped her voice on purpose. She's a few paces off with a stable full of mules between, and Pell tilts his head to catch what she says.

Just as Sefa begins, a mule in the end stall slams a hoof into its board, twice. Up the street the Red Sheaf takes up a new chorus with the door standing open. Pell can tell she's reading something out. It goes on in a steady run, like words read off a page, but under the stamping and the singing none of them reach him. Vosk's reply runs longer and lower still. Pell gets the rhythm of it, a man setting out terms one at a time. He hears one word, *paper*, and maybe *Nashkel* again, and nothing else. Whatever Sefa read off that crate, and whatever Vosk said back, stays down at the middle wagon.

Bram stands up from behind the wheel and turns to face the yard. Pell can see him plainly in the lantern light. His eyes go over the first wagon, the privy door and the gate, then settle on the woodstack and stay there.

Pell is already moving by then, one slow sidestep and then another, around the corner of the stack to where it meets the inn wall. The lamplight there stops well short of him. Bram looks into the shadow for a long moment. He has been staring into a candle, and the dark gives him nothing.

"Dunmore. If you're in this yard, step into the light. I'd sooner see you than guess at you."

It comes flat across the packed earth. In the stable a mule blows. No one answers.

Bram speaks to Vosk next, at the same carrying pitch, and Pell gets every word: the gate shut and barred, a lit lamp left by the door, and Bram himself bedded down *between the middle wagon and the stable wall, where I can see both.* So that's the night's plan, and anyone climbing the gate will know it too.

After that Sefa speaks again, quieter, and this time Pell catches the end of it because Vosk has stepped down from the wagon and is coming back toward the inn door with his lamp. *…I won't sign on for the Gate tonight…advice…before it goes back on the road.* So she isn't in yet.

Vosk stops a few paces from the door, close enough that Pell could count the buttons on his coat, with his back to the woodstack and his face to Sefa. He answers her in the same low voice. From here Pell can follow it: *tonight, not in the morning*, *from outside, with me standing by*, and last of all a question to Sefa: *Who did you have in mind? A priest at the Morninglord's house would ask me questions in front of half the town.*

Then he waits, with the lamp lit and his weight on the foot nearest the door, an arm's length past the corner where Pell is standing.

 Pell missed it. He strained after Sefa's lowered voice over the stamping and the open-door singing and lost every word of what she read off the crate, along with all but a word or two of Vosk's reply. Whatever is written on that seal, he's still the only one of the three who doesn't know it.
````

## Round 8

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 8 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram gives the shadow by the woodstack one more second, then lets it go. He's been on enough night watches to know when his eyes aren't going to give him what he wants.

"No answer," he says, to no one in particular. "So either he's gone over the fence, or he's a man who doesn't come when called. Both are worth knowing." He unslings the shield and settles it on his arm properly, the way he'd wear it for a watch rather than a walk. "Vosk. Before you go in — give me that lamp a moment."

If the factor hands it over, Bram walks the yard with it once, unhurried: along the dark side of the third wagon, round the back of the woodstack, a look at the top of the fence where a man might go over, the privy door pushed open with his foot. Not searching so much as letting the light into every place he couldn't see from the wheel. Then he brings the lamp back.

"Mistress Marrin. On advice — I'm no judge of mages, but I've walked past High Hedge a dozen times on the west road, and every carter on this stretch knows the name Thalantyr. Old conjurer, keeps to himself, sells potions to travellers who can pay and doesn't ask the town's leave for it. If there's a man within an hour of this yard who'd know what a Cowled seal and a humming box mean and keep his own counsel about it, that's the one I've heard of. Whether he'd come down here after dark for a stranger, I couldn't say. Most of what I know about him is that he doesn't like company." He shrugs. "That's the only name I've got. You lived in the Gate; you may know a better one."

To Vosk, as the man turns for the door: "Bar the gate. Lamp by the door. And Master Vosk — tell your boy Pim that if the tall one with the red neckcloth comes to the gate again tonight, he's to say nothing and come and fetch me. Not to argue with him. Just fetch me."

Then he takes up the place he named — between the middle wagon and the stable wall, back to the stone, where he can see the gate, the inn door, and the woodstack all at once — and sets his shield against his knee.

"I'll be here. Shout if you need me."

Sefa Marrin: “No one by name,” I admit. “I’m newly arrived. I’ll ask Orla whether there’s someone nearby who understands magical wards and can come tonight. Quietly—not a summons shouted across the common room.”

I glance at the covered crate. “I can promise discretion on my part, not that a scholar will ignore a danger if they find one. And I won’t agree to a fee on your behalf. If there’s someone available, I’ll bring you their terms first.”

To Bram, I add, “Condensation is the word. Cold wood draws moisture from the air. That explains the wet linen, not what’s making the wood cold.”

I head back inside to find Orla. When I can speak without interrupting an order, I ask quietly, “Do you know anyone in Beregost who’s knowledgeable about enchanted objects? We need advice on a sealed cargo in the yard—someone who could examine it here tonight without opening it. Master Vosk has agreed to that much.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell holds still for two beats after Bram's call, weighing it. Then he takes a short breath and steps out of the shadow at a measured pace, hands plainly away from his belt, so the movement reads before his face does.

"Easy," he says, low enough to carry to the three of them but not beyond. "I came out to see the yard quiet and found the talk wasn't for me. So I kept out of it." He stops just clear of the stack, a few strides off, and lets them see him plainly in the edge of the lamplight.

He looks to Bram first, then Vosk, then Sefa. "I'm headed north in the morning, Vosk, if you'd care to know." A small pause. "For now I don't sign anything. But I'd hear the offer plain, if there's one to hear."

The last he says in Vosk's direction, and he cocks his head to show he's listening now, not hiding.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Vosk looks at the lamp in his own hand, then at Bram, and holds it out without a word. Bram takes it by the ring.

He goes for the woodstack first, since that's where his eyes failed him. The light has barely reached the split ends of the logs when something moves at the corner where the stack meets the inn wall. It's slow, and it's meant to be seen. Pell Dunmore comes out into the lamplight with his hands held clear of his belt.

"Easy," he says, low enough that it reaches the three of them and no further. "I came out to see the yard quiet and found the talk wasn't for me. So I kept out of it." He stops a few strides off, just clear of the stack, and lets them get a good look at him.

Vosk's eyes go to the corner Pell came out of, then to the middle wagon. From that corner a man can see the whole length of the row. His mouth goes tight again and stays that way.

Bram carries the lamp on round the yard as he said he would. Behind the woodstack there's nothing but bark, a broken axe-haft and the mark of Pell's boots in the old straw. The third wagon has its canvas lashed tight, and every knot he can see is whole. The yard is closed in by the inn wall, the stable, and a board fence along the lane side, higher than a man's head and grey with weather. The fence is sound along most of its length. Past the tail of the third wagon one stretch has sagged where a post has rotted, and the top board sits a good foot lower than the rest. A man could get a hand over it from the lane and pull himself up. There are no fresh scrapes on it and nothing hanging from the nails. He pushes the privy door open with his boot. It's empty and smells the way privies do. He brings the lamp back.

He puts Thalantyr to Sefa while Vosk is still looking at Pell. At the name, Vosk turns his head.

"Thalantyr." He says it like a sum he doesn't trust. "In Nashkel they speak of him. They say that on the other side of the border he would have been in the Cowled cells forty years ago." He breathes out through his nose. "That is no reason against him. A man who doesn't ask the town's leave isn't going to tell the town my business either. If he comes tonight, he looks from outside, with me standing by."

"No one by name," Sefa says. "I'm newly arrived. I'll ask Orla whether there's someone nearby who understands magical wards and can come tonight. Quietly." She looks at the covered crate. "I can promise discretion on my part. I can't promise a scholar will ignore a danger if they find one. And I won't agree a fee on your behalf. If anyone's willing, I'll bring you their terms first."

"That is fair, Mistress Marrin." Vosk inclines his head. "Bring me the terms."

She turns to Bram. "Condensation is the word. Cold wood draws water out of the air. That explains the wet linen. It doesn't explain what's making the wood cold." Then she goes in through the yard door, and the noise of the common room comes out around her for a moment and shuts off.

Bram gives his orders about the gate, the lamp and Pim. Vosk nods to each one, and to the last one he adds, "Fetch, not argue. I'll tell him in those words." Bram goes to the place he chose, his back to the stable stone between the middle wagon and the wall. From there he can see the gate, the inn door and the woodstack.

Pell is still standing at the edge of the light. *I'm headed north in the morning, Vosk, if you'd care to know. For now I don't sign anything. But I'd hear the offer plain, if there's one to hear.*

Vosk shifts the ledger under his arm and looks at him for a long moment.

"Master… Dunmore." He says it slowly, as if he's writing it down. "Plain, then. Two gold a day and your food. Four days to the Gate if the weather holds, and we leave at first light. Half when we start, half at the Basilisk Gate. A guard's business is the outside of a wagon." He pauses. "And one term for you that I did not ask of the others. If you stand a watch for me, you stand it where Master Halloway can see you. I have just shown my cargo to two people, and I find there were three. I won't have that on the road."

He doesn't wait for an answer. He steps to the yard door, opens it a hand's width and calls into the passage. "Pim. The gate. Bar it, and then a lamp by this door, lit, and keep it lit." The stable boy comes out at a trot, ducking past Vosk's elbow. He kicks the stone away from the open leaf, swings the two boards shut and drops the bar into its brackets with a thump that echoes off the stable wall. Then he goes back in for the lamp.

"Think on it, Master Dunmore. Tell me before first light." Vosk goes in after Sefa.

---

Inside, the common room is louder than it was. The carters have started on the dice. Sefa finds Orla at the end of the counter, filling a row of cups, and waits until the last one is full before she asks.

Orla hears her out without stopping work. She wipes the jug's lip with her thumb and points it at Sefa.

"Enchanted things. There's two answers in Beregost, and you won't like either." She sets the jug down. "The temple. Kelddath would come, he's a decent man. But he'd bring two acolytes, and the whole town would have it at the well by breakfast. Your Amnian won't want that." She jerks her chin at the yard door, where Vosk has just come in with his ledger under his arm. "And Thalantyr, out at High Hedge. The west road, an hour's walk if you keep moving. He doesn't come down for anybody I've ever heard of. Folk go to him, by daylight, with coin in their hand. After dark his gate's shut, and I've never heard of anyone knocking on it twice." She picks the tray up. "Your quiet friend came in by that road this afternoon. Ask him how far it is."

She's already turning toward the dice table.

*(Out of game: no roll for Bram's walk round the yard, since checking a yard by lamplight is part of his job.)*

 *(Out of game: none of these four needed a roll. 1, 2 and 4 are just Bram talking and handling his own gear, with nothing uncertain about them. 3 is ordinary guard work in his own trade, so the walk got no card and turned up the sagging fence board past the third wagon and nothing else. Here are 1 and 2, which my last turn skipped over:)*

Bram watches the woodstack's shadow a moment longer, then turns away from it. "No answer," he says to nobody in particular. "So either he's gone over the fence, or he's a man who doesn't come when called. Both are worth knowing." He unslings the shield and works his arm into the straps the way he wears it on a night watch, and only then holds out his hand to Vosk for the lamp. Pell steps out into the light a moment later, just as the lamp swings toward the stack.

The yard is barred now and the lamp is lit by the door. Nothing else happens out here before Sefa comes back with Orla's answer, unless one of you makes it happen.
````
