````text
"That is fair, Mistress Marrin." Vosk inclines his head. "Bring me the terms."

She turns to Bram. "Condensation is the word. Cold wood draws water out of the air. That explains the wet linen. It doesn't explain what's making the wood cold." Then she goes in through the yard door. The noise of the common room comes out around her for a moment and shuts off.

Bram turns to Vosk. "Bar the gate. Lamp by the door. And Master Vosk, tell your boy Pim that if the tall one with the red neckcloth comes to the gate again tonight, he's to say nothing and come and fetch me. Not to argue with him. Just fetch me." Vosk nods to each order, and to the last he adds, "Fetch, not argue. I'll tell him in those words." Bram goes to a place between the middle wagon and the stable wall and sets his back to the stone. From there he can see the gate, the inn door and the woodstack. He sets his shield against his knee. "I'll be here. Shout if you need me."

Vosk shifts the ledger under his arm and looks at you for a long moment.

"Master… Dunmore." He says it slowly, as if he's writing it down. "Plain, then. Two gold a day and your food. Four days to the Gate if the weather holds, and we leave at first light. Half when we start, half at the Basilisk Gate. A guard's business is the outside of a wagon." He pauses. "And one term for you that I did not ask of the others. If you stand a watch for me, you stand it where Master Halloway can see you. I have just shown my cargo to two people, and I find there were three. I won't have that on the road."

He doesn't wait for an answer. He opens the yard door a hand's width and calls into the passage. "Pim. The gate. Bar it, and then a lamp by this door, lit, and keep it lit." The stable boy comes out at a trot, ducking past Vosk's elbow. He kicks the stone away from the open leaf and swings the two boards shut. He drops the bar into its brackets with a thump that echoes off the stable wall, then goes back in for the lamp.

"Think on it, Master Dunmore. Tell me before first light." Vosk goes in after Sefa.

The yard is barred now, and a lamp is lit by the door. Nothing else happens out here before Sefa comes back, unless one of you makes it happen.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore] [Sefa Marrin]

## Round 9

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 9 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram sets the lamp on the ground by his boot where it won't throw his own shadow onto the gate, and looks at Pell across the yard. He's not angry. He's been lied to by better men and worse.

"You heard me call you. Twice." He says it flat, like a fact about the weather. "A man who stands in the dark while another man calls his name has made a choice, and he's made it about me. I'll not hold it against you tonight. But Vosk's right, and I'd have said the same if he hadn't: on the road you walk where I can see you, or you don't walk with us." A beat. "You might be the best sword on this bench. I don't know. What I know is you listen well and you don't answer. On a quiet road that's a virtue. On this one, I need to know which way you'll jump before the jumping starts."

He nods toward the tail of the third wagon. "Since you're standing there and not signing anything — the fence past that wagon's sagged a foot where the post's gone. That's where I'd come over if I were Harn. Pim's barred the gate, which keeps out honest men. If you want to be useful without taking Vosk's coin, drag a couple of those split logs over and stack them under that board so a man can't get a hand over without knocking something down. It'll make noise, and noise is all I want out of it."

He settles back against the stone, shield against his knee, sword where his hand falls on it.

"After that, go in and sleep if you like. I'll wake you before first light either way." He glances at the inn door. "And if you're still deciding — Vosk's a tired man carrying a box he's afraid of, and he's told us more truth tonight than most factors tell in a season. That counts for something with me. Whether it counts for anything with you is your business."

Pell Dunmore: Pell listens through Vosk's terms without moving, but something quickens behind his eyes at the last part.

"I'll take a watch, and not behind the wagons," he says. "If I take it. Plain suits me. Two gold, food, and the work is outside the canvas. I don't need to breathe on the crate."

He lets that lie a moment, then adds more quietly, to Bram: "When a man says my name like that, he's finished trusting me for the evening. I'd rather earn the rest back than talk it out of him." He looks toward the barred gate, then back to Bram. "You're a steady one. You can have my company here for a while, if it doesn't put your shield work off. I'm not signing by lamplight, but I'm not climbing over, either."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram says his piece across the yard, level and unhurried, the lamp at his boot throwing its light low across the packed earth and away from the gate. In the stable a mule shifts its weight and goes quiet again. Up the street the Red Sheaf's song falls apart in laughter.

Pell hears him out. When he answers he speaks to the inn door first, where Vosk went in. The latch is down and nobody is behind it to hear.

"I'll take a watch, and not behind the wagons. If I take it. Plain suits me. Two gold, food, and the work is outside the canvas. I don't need to breathe on the crate."

Then, quieter, to Bram: "When a man says my name like that, he's finished trusting me for the evening. I'd rather earn the rest back than talk it out of him." His eyes go to the barred gate and come back. "You're a steady one. You can have my company here for a while, if it doesn't put your shield work off. I'm not signing by lamplight, but I'm not climbing over, either."

He hasn't said a word about the logs, and they're still stacked by the door.

The yard door opens before either of them says more. Pim backs out with a second lamp in one hand and a bowl in the other, shouldering the door wide with his hip. He hooks the lamp on the nail beside the frame, checks the flame, then looks at the bowl as if he isn't sure how he ended up holding it.

"Master Vosk says I'm to fetch and not argue," he tells Bram, all in one breath, the way a boy repeats an order so he won't lose it. "If the tall one comes. Fetch, not argue." Then he turns to Pell and holds the bowl out at arm's length. The stew in it has gone grey and set at the edges. "And Mistress Tarnwick says this is yours, master, and it's three silver whether you eat it or not, and she's not keeping it warm for a man who goes out the back."

He waits there with his arm out and the bowl steady, plainly more afraid of Orla than of either man in the yard.

*(Out of game: Vosk had already gone inside, so only Bram heard Pell answer the terms. Pell can still take it to Vosk himself before first light.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) This isn't a roll. Pell said "If I take it" and "I'm not signing by lamplight," and Vosk had already gone inside, so nobody was there to take his answer yet. He can tell Vosk himself before first light.

2) No roll was needed. Pell offered to keep Bram company in the yard and he's out there now, as the turn shows.

3) Pell's paragraph didn't answer Bram's request about the logs, so I left them where they are rather than decide for him. Whether he stacks them is up to Pell's player.
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 9 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “Thank you. I’d rather not learn why they only knock once.”

I cross to Vosk and speak quietly.

“Orla confirms Thalantyr, but he’s an hour away, his gate is shut after dark, and she’s never known him to come into town. I won’t promise to fetch him tonight.”

“She also says Kelddath at the temple is a decent man and would come. She expects acolytes and gossip; that’s a concern, not a certainty. We could send a sealed note asking whether he can consult here privately, without naming your cargo in it.”

I pause. “Would you authorise that? I’ll carry the request, but I won’t invent a harmless explanation for the crate to secure his silence. If you’d rather refuse, I’ll leave the matter there—and decline the journey.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla is already half-turned toward the dice table, and she gives Sefa's thanks a short dry look over her shoulder. "Nobody's told me why. Nobody's gone back twice to find out, either." Then she's gone into the noise with the tray.

Vosk has taken his old place at the hearth end of the long table. The ledger is open again, but he isn't writing in it. He looks up as Sefa reaches him and hears her out: the hour's walk, the shut gate, Kelddath, and the note that wouldn't name the cargo. At *decline the journey* he closes the ledger over one finger and keeps it there.

"That is a fair offer, Mistress Marrin, and you have put it honestly. I will answer it the same way." He keeps his voice under the rattle of the dice. "No. Not the temple."

He waits while a carter pushes past behind the bench, then goes on.

"I don't refuse because of the acolytes. Kelddath Ormlyr is not only the Morninglord's priest. In this town, when two smiths quarrel over a boundary or a debt, they take it to him and abide by what he says. Ask any carter on the Way. A note from you is a note to the man who keeps Beregost's peace. It says that an Amnian in the Burning Wizard's yard has something on his wagon that frightens him." His mouth tightens. "A decent man, as Mistress Tarnwick says. A decent man in his place might feel he has to see the paper, and the names on it, and then decide whether the thing is going to leave his town at all. I can't afford that risk, and the man I carry it for won't forgive it."

He looks past her toward the yard door once, then back.

"Thalantyr I would have taken, because he answers to nobody. But you are right that he won't come, and I won't wait for him." He breathes out slowly through his nose. "So you decline, and I don't hold it against you. You looked when the others only listened, and you told me plainly what you couldn't see. Few people would have done as much. If you change your mind before the wagons roll, the terms are as I gave them."

He takes the finger out of the ledger and lays his hand flat on the closed cover.

"I'll ask one thing of you, though I have no right to it. You read that seal aloud. Will you keep what it says to yourself while I am still in this town?"
````

## Round 10

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 10 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram almost smiles at the bowl.

"Take it, Dunmore. She'll charge you for the walk out here next." To Pim, with a nod at the lamp on its nail: "Good. That's where I want it. Now get to your straw. If anything comes over that fence tonight you'll hear it before you see it, and all I want from you is your feet on the stairs and Vosk's door."

When the boy's gone, Bram looks at the woodstack, then at Pell, and doesn't ask twice. He gets up himself, stiff from the road, and carries four split logs across the yard two at a time. He wedges them under the sagging board past the third wagon, not neatly — balanced, so a hand pulling on the fence from the lane side brings the top one down onto the others with a clatter. He tests it with a nudge of his boot, nods at the sound it makes, and comes back.

"There. Honest men use the gate. Everyone else announces themselves." He settles against the stone again, shield to his knee.

"You can have the watch till Sefa's back, if you want it, and I'll not call it signing anything. I've no quarrel with a man who'd rather earn trust than talk it. Just understand that I don't give it out by lamplight either." He tips his head toward the inn door. "She's gone to find a mage who'll keep his mouth shut, in a smiths' town, after dark. If she manages it, we'll all learn something tonight. If she doesn't, I walk north at dawn with a humming box and two strangers, which is about how most of my runs have started." He closes his eyes for the span of a breath and opens them again on the gate. "Eat your stew. It's paid for."

Pell Dunmore: Pell fishes three silver out of his pouch before he takes the bowl, and presses them into Pim's palm.

"There. Bowl and all. Tell Mistress Tarnwick I don't expect it kept warm, and thank her for sending it." He drops a copper on top. "For the errand."

He waits until the boy's gone, then looks across the yard at Bram.

"I heard you call. I stayed in the shadow until the lamp came round, and that's on me — not against you, but you're right to mark it." He sets the bowl on the bench by the watering trough, where Bram can see both hands. "I said I'd take a watch, and I meant it. If we walk together, I'll walk where you can see me."

He crosses to the woodstack, chooses two sound logs, and drags them one after another to the sagging boards beyond the third wagon. He stacks them so they lean against the fence just under its lowest handhold — enough that anyone putting weight there will send one tumbling with a clatter on the packed earth. Then he returns to the yard light, picks up his bowl, and settles with his back to the stable wall, not far from Bram.

"I'll sleep when I'm done eating," he says quietly. "Wake me at first light, or before it if the gate moves." He eats the cold stew without complaint.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pim's arm is getting tired. He looks from Bram to Pell, and by the time Bram has finished speaking, Pell already has his purse open. Three silver go into the boy's palm and a copper lands on top of them. The bowl changes hands.

"Not kept warm, and thank her for sending it," Pim says to himself, closing his fist over the coins. He stares at the copper for a moment as if he isn't sure it's meant for him. Then he decides it is and puts it in a different pocket from the silver. "Yes, master. Feet on the stairs, Master Vosk's door." He ducks back through the yard door, and the noise of the common room swells for a moment and cuts off when it shuts.

Bram is on his feet before the latch drops, stiff in the knees and slow about it. He takes the logs from the stack two at a time and carries four of them past the tail of the third wagon to the sagging stretch of fence. He doesn't stack them neatly. He leans them, bottom ends dug into the old straw and top ends resting against each other just under the low board, so they hold together only because nothing has touched them yet. He nudges the pile with his boot. The top log slides and knocks against the others with a dry wooden clack that bounces off the stable wall. A mule in the end stall jerks its head up and snorts. Bram sets the log back and walks back to his place.

Pell has set the bowl down on the edge of the watering trough by the stable door, where the lantern lights it and his hands. He says his piece across the yard while Bram is still coming back. Then he goes to the woodstack himself and takes two more logs, picking sound ones, and drags them one at a time across the packed earth to the fence. He leans them against the boards right under the place where a hand coming over from the lane would grip. Now anyone climbing has to get past his two before he reaches Bram's four. It isn't a wall. It's a heap of firewood ready to fall over and wake half the inn, which is the whole point.

He comes back into the light, picks up the bowl and sits down with his back to the stable wall, a few feet from Bram, where Bram can see him. The stew has gone grey at the edges and stiff with cold fat. Pell eats it anyway.

The yard settles. The lamp by the door burns steadily on its nail. Vosk's candle-lamp sits low by Bram's boot and lights the ground more than the gate. Up the street the Red Sheaf is quieter now, just voices with no song. The barred gate holds, and the logs past the third wagon lean where the two of them put them.

Nothing more happens in the yard for now, unless one of you makes it happen.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) No roll was needed: leaning six logs against a fence so they fall when someone climbs is routine work in Bram's own trade, and nothing about it was uncertain.

2) No roll was needed, since this is just talk. One correction: Bram offered Pell the watch until Sefa gets back. He didn't take it himself.

3) No roll was needed for paying Pim or for Pell stacking his two logs. Neither had any chance of going wrong, and the three silver are Orla's payment for the stew, which Pim is carrying to her.
````

He closes his eyes for the span of a breath and opens them again on the gate. "Eat your stew. It's paid for."

````text
You come back into the light, pick up the bowl and sit down with your back to the stable wall, a few feet from Bram, where he can see you. The stew has gone grey at the edges and stiff with cold fat. You eat it anyway.

The yard settles. The lamp by the door burns steadily on its nail. Vosk's candle-lamp sits low by Bram's boot and lights the ground more than the gate. Up the street the Red Sheaf is quieter now, just voices with no song. The barred gate holds, and the logs past the third wagon lean where the two of you put them.

Nothing more happens in the yard for now, unless one of you makes it happen.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 10 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “I won’t spread it about, Master Vosk. Nor will I go to the temple behind your back merely because you refused.”

I meet his eyes. “But if someone comes to harm, or there’s a clear danger to people here, I’ll tell whoever can help what I know. That’s the promise I can give you.”

I ease back from the table. “I hope your journey proves quieter than your evening.”

Then I return to the yard to speak to Bram, and Pell if he’s still there.

“No expert tonight. Thalantyr’s gate is shut, and Vosk won’t involve Kelddath because of his authority in town. I’ve declined the work.”

I look at Bram. “If anything changes while you’re watching, wake me. Upstairs, second door on the left. I don’t know what’s in that crate, but I won’t leave you alone with someone hurt.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Vosk hears her out with his hand still flat on the closed ledger. When she gets to *if someone comes to harm*, he doesn't argue. He nods slowly, once.

"That is a fair promise, Mistress Marrin, and an honest one. I would think less of you if you had given me more." He takes his hand off the ledger. "If it comes to harm, I'll not hold you to silence. I'd not hold myself to it either."

At her last words his mouth moves. It isn't quite a smile. "Quieter would suit me. Good night."

He opens the ledger again as she stands up. By the time she's past the end of the long table he has picked up his pen, though he hasn't written anything yet.

The common room is louder than when she came in. The carters' dice game has spread over two tables, and someone is losing badly enough to complain about it. Pim slips past her in the passage toward the counter with his fist closed tight around something. He doesn't look up. Sefa lifts the latch of the yard door and the noise falls away behind her.

The yard has changed a little since she left it. The gate is shut, and its bar sits in the brackets. A second lamp burns on the nail beside the door, so the doorway is the brightest spot in the yard. Vosk's candle-lamp sits low on the ground by Bram's boot. He's where he said he'd be, with his back to the stable stone between the middle wagon and the wall and his shield against his knee. A few feet from him Pell sits against the stable wall in plain sight, with an earthenware bowl in his lap. Past the tail of the third wagon, at the edge of what the light reaches, there's a heap of split logs at the foot of the fence that wasn't there before. They lean against each other under the low board.

Sefa says her piece across the packed earth: no expert tonight, Thalantyr's gate shut, Vosk unwilling to bring in Kelddath because of where the priest stands in this town, and her own refusal of the work. Then she tells Bram where she'll be. *Upstairs, second door on the left.* And she says she won't leave him alone with someone hurt.

In the stable a mule shifts its weight and blows. Up the street the Red Sheaf has gone quiet apart from voices.

*(Out of game: Bram and Pell are in their own scene, so their answers come from their players there.)*
````

## Round 11

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 11 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram watches Pell drag the two logs over and set them under the hand-grip, and says nothing about it, which from him is as good as a nod.

He eases down against the stone, finds the place where the wall takes the weight of the mail, and lets his breathing slow. The shield stays against his knee. His right hand lies open on his thigh a hand's width from the hilt, the way it's lain through a few hundred nights like this one.

"Dunmore." Quiet, not turning his head. "Two things, and then I'll shut up and let you eat. First: if it's Harn over that fence, he's a man who's seen the box and come back for it, and I'd like to know why before anyone bleeds. So if he comes, I want him talking, not running. Stand between him and the fence, not between him and me." A pause. "Second: if it's not Harn — if it's something that doesn't use the gate or the fence — you get Vosk and Sefa out the front door and up the street, and you don't wait for me. I've no idea what a Cowled seal keeps in, and I'd sooner find out alone than with three."

He lets his eyes rest on the gate.

"That's all. I'll wake you."

Then he does what caravan guards do, which is mostly nothing: counts the lamps, listens to the mules, marks the moment the Red Sheaf goes quiet, and waits for the yard door to open on Sefa or for the logs to fall.

Sefa Marrin: Before going back inside, I glance toward the fence.

“Those logs weren’t there when I left. Did one of you put them there?”

I stay by the doorway while I wait for an answer, looking at the low board above the pile. “If not, someone’s been busy remarkably close to your watch.”

Pell Dunmore: Pell eats the stew without hurrying, his back to the stable wall and his eyes moving on their own rhythm — gate, fence, shadows, gate again. The bowl he balances on one knee, and between spoonfuls he listens past the yard's small sounds: the joint-creak of the wagons cooling, the rats under the straw, the mules' slow shifts of weight.

Once he has set the empty bowl aside he says, quietly enough that it stays within the yard, "I was past the middle wagon earlier and heard the mules stamping when you put your weight on those logs. They're sharper than most watchmen." It's not idleness; he is checking whether Bram made anything of that, and giving the man a chance to speak if he wants to.

Then he folds his arms and lets the watch hang between them, seemingly at ease. He'll wait for Sefa or for movement.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram says his two things to the side of Pell's head, low and even, without taking his eyes off the gate. He wants Harn talking, not running, and Pell between him and the fence. If it's something else, Pell is to get Vosk and Sefa out the front door and up the street without waiting for him. Then he settles his back into the stone until the mail stops pressing wrong, lays his right hand open on his thigh near the hilt, and goes quiet.

The yard door is still open a hand's width behind Sefa, and the lamp on its nail puts her in the brightest spot in the yard. Her eyes go past the wagons to the fence. The heap of split logs at the foot of the sagging board is just inside the light. They lean against each other the way logs never fall on their own.

"Those logs weren't there when I left. Did one of you put them there?" She stays in the doorway, looking at the low board above the pile. "If not, someone's been busy remarkably close to your watch."

Pell finishes the stew. It's cold, and he eats it to the bottom anyway, scraping the bowl. He sets it down on the straw beside him and says, quietly enough that it stays inside the fence, that the mules stamped when Bram put his weight on those logs, and that they're sharper than most watchmen. He says it to Bram. Sefa hears it too, standing in the doorway.

Then Pell folds his arms and leans back against the stable wall.

The yard goes back to its small noises. A wagon creaks as the wood cools. Something small runs under the straw near the privy. In the end stall a mule shifts its weight from one hind foot to the other and sighs through its nose like a tired old man. Up the street the Red Sheaf is still going, voices and now and then a laugh, with no song yet. The gate's bar sits in its brackets. The six logs lean where they were put.

Sefa's question is still waiting in the doorway for whichever of them answers it.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) No roll was needed. Bram told Pell both sets of orders in the scene; whether Pell takes them is up to Pell's player.
2) No roll was needed. Settling in for a watch is routine work in Bram's trade, and he's against the stone now, shield at his knee and hand near the hilt.
3) No roll was needed, since it's just a question. Sefa asked it from the doorway, and it's waiting for Bram's and Pell's players to answer.
4) No roll was needed. Pell finished the stew while he watched, and so far there's nothing at the gate or the fence for him to notice.
````

## Round 12

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Sefa, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 12 — one paragraph per character in this scene.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't get up.

"Mine. Four of them. Dunmore's the two under the hand-grip." He tips his head at the fence without looking away from the gate. "Post's rotted past the third wagon and the board sits a foot low. Anyone coming over from the lane puts a hand on it and brings the lot down. It's not a wall. It's a bell."

Now he does look at her, and at the open door behind her.

"You've been a while. Did Orla give you a name, or just the price of asking?" He shifts the shield against his knee. "If there's someone coming to look at that box tonight, I want to know how many and by which door, so I don't put a javelin in a mage. And if there isn't — say so, and I'll stop hoping for it and get on with the watch." A beat. "Shut that door behind you either way. Vosk pays for the yard, not the heat."

Sefa Marrin: “Bram’s work, then?” I glance between them. “All right. I only wanted to be sure you knew they were there.”

I pull my cloak closer. “Good night, both of you. Wake me if you need me.”

I head inside, collect what remains of my supper if it hasn’t been cleared, and then go up to my room. Once inside, I set down my pack, check that the room is empty and bolt the door. Before settling to sleep, I sit on the bed with my prayer beads in my hands.

“Lantern, watch over those keeping watch.”

Then I take off my armour and get ready for bed, keeping my book and clothes within reach.

Pell Dunmore: Bram answers Sefa first, without getting up. "Both of us. The fence post's gone, and a man can get a hand over the boards there. The logs aren't a wall. They're a bell."

He looks across at you then, one eyebrow raised. You can see he's waiting for you to add your own piece of the truth, if you care to.

You give it. "I did the same for the same reason, on my own way back from the stack." You say it plainly, meeting her eye, because there's no profit in making a mystery of it. "It'll clatter before anyone gets a boot on the gate-side. That's all." You settle again, arms folded, and leave the rest to the quiet.

Sefa watches you a moment longer, as if your saying so has let her set down a piece she was still carrying. Then she steps down into the yard, leaving the door open only a hand's width, just as it was before. She moves to the pile and bows to you as the wizard Thalantyr does, with a little of the barn cat in the way she holds her gaze.

"The wizard's name is Thalantyr, and he asked Orla to keep my half-bowl back. He has the gout and knows it." She manages the whisper so it reaches you both and carries no further. You catch it, but only because you were listening for it. "He won't be long, and he won't come through the front. And if I'd wanted to discuss your charred bark, I'd have waited for full dark. But tell me, Bram: you measure men by the set of their shoulders. What would it look like if the ward isn't one I can open? Or the box?"

Then a silence comes down over the yard, the way a cat's arrival quiets a room. You don't hear the inn's front door open. The alley cats go quiet one by one. Then a shadow flows out of the narrow space between the inn and its neighbour's wall, and he's there — a man dark as a stack of old books, in a flat hat and a long dark-green coat with the collar up. He doesn't clatter. He just walks down the yard to the low place in the fence and stands there, looking at the logs, while the alley behind him purrs back into cat-song, one voice and then another.

Then he glances over at the three of you, and his glasses catch the lamp. He waits, visible but not advancing. Things feel the way they do before morning prayers in an unfriendly shrine.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram stays where he is against the stone, shield at his knee and his eyes on the gate.

"Mine. Four of them. Dunmore's the two under the hand-grip." He tips his head toward the fence. "Post's rotted past the third wagon and the board sits a foot low. Anyone coming over from the lane puts a hand on it and brings the lot down. It's not a wall. It's a bell."

Then he looks up at her in the doorway, and at the warm strip of common room behind her. He asks whether Orla gave her a name or just the price of asking. He wants to know how many are coming and by which door, so he doesn't put a javelin in a mage, and if nobody is coming she should say so. Last, he tells her to shut the door either way, because Vosk is paying for the yard and not the heat.

"Bram's work, then?" Sefa looks from one man to the other. "All right. I only wanted to be sure you knew they were there." She pulls her cloak closer. "Good night, both of you. Wake me if you need me."

Pell gives her his piece before she turns. "I did the same for the same reason, on my way back from the stack." He says it plainly and meets her eye. "It'll clatter before anyone gets a boot down on this side. That's all." Then he folds his arms again and settles back against the stable wall.

Sefa goes in. The yard door swings to behind her and the latch drops. In the yard there's the lamp on its nail, the mules, and the low murmur of the Red Sheaf up the street.

---

The common room is louder than when she left it. The carters' dice game has taken over the far table where the three of them ate. The jug, the cups and her half-eaten bread are gone, and a man in a leather jerkin has his elbow where her bowl was. Orla is at the counter with her back turned, pulling ale. Up at the hearth end Vosk sits over his open ledger. The pen is in his hand and still dry.

The stairs creak at the third step and at the seventh. At the top the passage smells of tallow and old rushes. The second door on the left opens on a narrow room: a bed with a straw tick and two wool blankets, a stool, a washstand with a chipped jug, and one shutter latched against the Coast Way. Sefa looks under the bed, behind the door and into the corner beside the washstand. There's nobody in the room. The bolt is a heavy iron one on the inside, a little stiff, and it goes home with a solid knock, as Orla said it would.

She sits on the edge of the bed with the beads between her fingers. The cracked one comes round under her thumb.

"Lantern, watch over those keeping watch."

Through the floorboards the dice game cheers someone's throw. Through the shutter a smithy down the road rings three last strokes on cooling iron, and then it's quiet.

She unbuckles the studded leather and lays it over the stool. The book goes on the blanket beside her pillow and her clothes go where her hand can find them in the dark.

---

The night goes on from here: Bram's watch by the stone, Pell against the wall, Sefa behind her bolt. Tell me if you want to jump ahead, and to when, or stop at any hour along the way.

*(Out of game: I can't use the second half of Pell's paragraph, where Sefa comes back with word of Thalantyr and the man in the green coat arrives, because Sefa's player sent her up to bed and nobody went for Thalantyr. So none of that happened.)*
*(Out of game: Bram, Sefa already gave the yard her answer when she came out a quarter of an hour ago: no expert tonight, Thalantyr's gate is shut, Vosk won't have Kelddath in, and she has turned the work down.)*
````

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**Bram Halloway, out of game:** ? Just so I've got it right: Sefa told us earlier — no expert tonight, Thalantyr's shut, Vosk won't have the Lathander priest in, and she's not taking the work. I'll play it as heard. One more thing — did she say whether she's still walking north in the morning, with or without pay? Bram lets the latch drop and sits with it a while. "So that's that," he says to the dark, quiet. "No mage, no priest, and the healer's out. Two of us and a tired Amnian, if you sign." He doesn't make it a question. "I'll take it till the Red Sheaf shuts and the street goes dead. Then you take it till the sky greys, and I'll have an hour's sleep against the wheel. Shake me if the logs go, if the gate moves, or if that box does anything a box shouldn't — sound, cold, light, I don't care which. Don't wait to be sure." He settles his shoulders into the stone, shield to his knee, hand open by the hilt, and keeps his eyes on the gate. [Onward] — to the end of the Red Sheaf's noise and the change of watch, unless the yard has something to say first.

**The DM, to Bram only:** Yes, you have it right. Sefa said no expert tonight, Thalantyr's gate is shut, Vosk won't have Kelddath in, and she has declined the work.

On the morning she said nothing either way. She didn't say whether she's walking north, paid or unpaid, or staying in Beregost. The closest she came was at supper, when she said she'd just come down that road from the Gate, wasn't eager to turn straight round, but would listen to an offer. She listened and turned it down. Her last words in the yard were about tonight: wake her if you need her, upstairs, second door on the left, and she won't leave you alone with someone hurt. What she does at first light is for Sefa's player to say, so Bram doesn't know yet.

One small thing about your lines: Pell still hasn't told Vosk yes or no. So "two of us, if you sign" is accurate, and whether Pell takes the second half of the watch is his player's answer. I'll play your watch orders and the move toward the change of watch in the next round, alongside whatever Pell's player sends. **What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram takes the answer the way he takes most things, with a short nod to nobody.

He doesn't add to his watch orders; they're said. He only shifts the shield so the rim won't dig into his knee over the long hours, and lets the yard settle around him — gate, lamp, logs, the mules' breathing, the thin noise from up the street.

"Red Sheaf shuts, you take it," he says once more, low, to be sure Pell heard it the first time. "Till then, sleep if you can."

Pell Dunmore: Pell watches the yard door close and lets the quiet come back down over the yard. He doesn't repeat what Bram already said about the fence, and he doesn't fill the dark with talk. He sits with his back to the stable wall, one knee drawn up, and keeps his own watch in the still way of a man used to long hours and no company.

The night goes on small and ordinary. Pell follows the sounds: the settling wagons, the mules, the cat voices, the last laughter from the Red Sheaf. He tests the line of shadow past the privy and the fence and rests his eyes on the gate between each slow circuit. Now and then he shifts his weight without a sound, to keep his limbs from going cold.

He lets Bram carry the talk if Bram wants it; otherwise he saves his words for something that matters. He'll stand watch as long as Bram lets him and wake sharp if anything moves.

[Onward — jump ahead through the night, a couple of hours at a time, until something changes or first light]
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The yard settles around the two of them.

For the first hour and more there's very little to hear. The lamp on its nail by the yard door burns with a steady flame. Down at Bram's boot, Vosk's candle-lamp gutters lower and lower until it's only a bead of light at the bottom of the glass. In the stable the mules shift their weight now and then, and the end-stall mule blows through its nose. The middle wagon creaks once as the wood cools. Somewhere along the lane behind the fence two cats start a quarrel and drop it again.

Up the street the Red Sheaf goes on longer than the Burning Wizard does. Through the yard door the common room thins out: the dice game breaks up with a groan and a laugh, boots go up the stairs, and the third and seventh steps creak over and over as the carters climb to the loft. After a while there's only Orla's voice, short, then a bar dropping somewhere inside, and the house goes dark except for the lamp outside its door. The Red Sheaf keeps going another hour. Then a door bangs up the street, three or four voices carry along the Coast Way, saying good night badly, and the street goes quiet.

That's near midnight. The cold has come down properly now, and the packed earth has a damp shine in the lamplight.

Bram has kept his eyes on the gate the whole time. He hasn't moved much beyond easing the shield off his knee. He has had nothing to watch but a quiet yard.

When the voices have faded down the street, Bram looks over at Pell, gives him one short nod, and works himself down against the spokes of the middle wagon's wheel with the shield across his lap. His breathing slows. Within a few minutes it's a soldier's sleep, light and even, the kind that wakes at the wrong sound.

The watch is Pell's.

The next stretch is the long one. The candle-lamp by Bram's boot goes out on its own in a thread of tallow smoke. Only the lamp by the door is left, and it lights the doorway, the woodstack and the first wagon. Past the third wagon the fence is a grey line, and the logs at its foot are just shapes. The cold works into the stable wall at Pell's back. Once, when he shifts his shoulders against the stone, he feels the faint thrum again, carried through the wall from the wagons, steady as a sleeping animal. Then the mule in the end stall stamps and he loses it. The Coast Way is empty. No smithy rings. Once a dog barks far off toward the south end of town and stops.

A while after the middle of the night, deep in the small hours, the lane behind the fence speaks.

It's a foot, then another. They're slow and uneven, and they belong to a man trying to walk quietly who isn't good at it. A stone turns under a boot with a gritty scrape, and someone breathes out sharp through his teeth. The steps come along the lane from the direction of the Coast Way, on the far side of the boards, past the privy corner and past the tail of the third wagon. Then they stop.

They've stopped right behind the low stretch, where the post has rotted and the top board sits a foot lower than the rest.

For a moment there's nothing. Then the board creaks softly, the way wood does when a hand closes on it from the other side and tests it without putting any weight on it yet. Over the fence, faint in the cold air, comes the smell of stale ale and pipe smoke.

The six logs are still leaning where they were put. Bram is asleep against the wheel, a few feet from Pell. No one has climbed yet.

 *(Out of game: Pell's watch didn't need a roll. Keeping watch is routine work in his trade, so his passive Perception covered it, and that's how he heard the man in the lane before anyone touched the fence.)*
````

````text
(Round 13 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa sleeps.

For a while the inn goes on under her. The dice game cheers one last throw and breaks up. Boots go up the stairs to the loft in twos and threes, and the third and seventh steps creak under each of them. Someone in the passage outside tries the door across the hall, the one whose bolt doesn't work, finds the room he wants is further along, and goes off muttering. Then Orla's short voice comes from below, a bar drops into its brackets somewhere downstairs, and the house goes quiet.

The Red Sheaf lasts longer. Its voices come faintly through the shutter, and a little before midnight a door bangs up the street. Three or four men call good night to each other along the Coast Way, badly, and then the street is empty too.

After that there is only the cold working into the room. The straw tick rustles when she turns over. The wool blankets smell faintly of woodsmoke and of whoever slept under them last. One of the carters in the loft snores, steady as a saw. Once in the small hours a dog barks far off toward the south end of town, stops, and doesn't start again. The cracked bead of the string on her pillow has settled under her hand.

Nobody knocks. No alarm comes up the stairs, and nothing reaches her through the floor or the shutter. It is deep in the night now, hours yet before the shutter's cracks turn grey, and the bolt sits where she drove it home.

*(Out of game: Sefa is asleep at the same hour as the yard. Whether she sleeps through to dawn depends on what happens down there next, so her night stops here for now.)*
````

## Round 14

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

*Bram Halloway pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 14 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram sleeps the way he's slept against a hundred wheels — one hand on the shield rim, the other fallen open near the hilt, breathing slow. The hum comes up through the spokes into his back, and he doesn't notice it any more than he'd notice a cart's rattle; it's just the sound the wagon makes.

 — to whatever wakes him: Pell's hand, the logs, the gate, the box, or the grey in the sky.

Pell Dunmore: Pell doesn't move quickly. He turns his head toward Bram, covers the few feet between them on the balls of his feet, and puts two fingers on the guard's boot.

"Company," he breathes, close enough that the word doesn't leave the yard. "Fence, past the third wagon. A man testing the low board."

He straightens into a low crouch, then points once — not toward the fence itself, but to the shadow along the stable wall and the dark between the privy and the wagons. His meaning is plain: *say the word and I'll cut off his way back to the lane.*

He waits for Bram's eyes to open and his hand to find the hilt, then moves silent and low, keeping out of the direct lamp glow. His hand does not go to his shortsword yet; he wants Harn talking, same as Bram. But he chooses a place between the third wagon and the privy, where a man dropping over the fence would have to get past him to run.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Two fingers on the boot are enough. Bram's breathing changes before his eyes open, the way it does with men who've slept on watch for years. Then his eyes are open and on Pell's face, and Pell's breath of a word reaches him: *Company. Fence, past the third wagon. A man testing the low board.*

Pell points once, at the dark along the stable wall and the gap between the privy and the wagons, and goes.

He keeps low and stays out of the lamplight. The packed earth is damp with cold and makes no sound under the balls of his feet. He passes the middle wagon's tailboard, where the hum comes up faint through the boards, then the third wagon's lashed canvas, then into the dark between its tail and the privy wall. It's about thirty feet from where Bram is sitting. He stops in a crouch with the privy at his back. The sagging stretch of fence is ten feet off to his left, the six logs are grey shapes at its foot, and the lane is right behind it. A man who comes over there and wants to go back the way he came has to get past Pell first. His hand stays off the shortsword.

On the far side of the boards the man breathes out, long and unsteady, like someone working up to something. The smell of stale ale and pipe smoke is stronger here.

Then the low board takes his weight.

It groans. A hand comes over the top, then a second one, pale knuckles in the dark and a frayed cuff. A head and shoulders come up after them, hauled the last foot by a man who isn't as strong as he used to be and has had a drink too many. He's tall. Even in this light the cloth knotted at his throat is darker than his shirt, and it might be red. He hangs there with his elbows hooked over the board and stares at the yard. He doesn't look into the dark at his feet. He looks at the lamp burning on its nail by the yard door, and then along the row of wagons to the middle one.

He swings a leg over.

His boot comes down on the inside looking for something to stand on, and it finds Pell's two logs. They go out from under him, slide into Bram's four, and all six come down together. The clatter of split oak on packed earth bangs off the stable wall, much louder than it ought to be at this hour. In the end stall a mule screams and kicks its boards twice. Somewhere out in the lane a dog starts barking.

The man comes down with them. He lands on one hip and one hand among the rolling logs, half in the yard with a leg still hooked on the board. "Gods' *teeth*," he hisses, and it's more pain than surprise. He gets his leg free and pushes himself up onto one knee. There's a knife in a sheath at his belt. He hasn't touched it. He's still looking toward the middle wagon and the lamp, breathing hard, and he hasn't looked round. He doesn't know that ten feet behind his right shoulder someone is crouched between him and the fence.

Behind the canvas, the middle wagon's hum goes on as steady as ever.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) No roll was needed: Pell's touch woke Bram and he's awake at the wheel. I didn't narrate his hand going to the hilt because that line came from Pell's paragraph, and what Bram does now that he's awake is for Bram's player to say.

2) No roll was needed: moving quietly through a dark yard is routine work in Pell's trade, and the man outside was drunk, behind a board fence and looking at the lamp. Pell got into position unheard, about ten feet behind Harn and between him and the fence.

3) No roll was needed: it was a silent gesture. Bram saw Pell point at the stable wall and the gap by the privy before Pell moved off.
````

Then your eyes are open and on Pell's face, close above you, and his breath of a word reaches you: *"Company. Fence, past the third wagon. A man testing the low board."*

````text
He straightens into a low crouch and points once: not at the fence itself, but at the dark along the stable wall and the gap between the privy and the wagons. Then he goes.

He keeps low and stays out of the lamplight, and his feet make no sound on the damp packed earth. You watch him pass the middle wagon's tailboard and the third wagon's lashed canvas, then lose him into the dark between its tail and the privy wall, about thirty feet from where you sit.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa is asleep under the two wool blankets with the beads loose under her hand. Down below, behind the house, six split logs hit packed earth all at once. A mule screams, its hooves bang on stable boards twice, and out in the lane a dog starts barking.

The noise reaches her room through a passage, a door and the house's thick old walls. Her shutter faces the Coast Way and the yard is on the far side of the house. By the time the sound gets to her it's blurred, a far-off wooden thump with an animal cry somewhere inside it.

Sefa sleeps through it. She turns onto her side and the straw tick rustles. She pulls the blanket up over her shoulder without waking. The dog barking in the lane becomes part of whatever she is dreaming, and then it fades.

Under her hand the cracked bead is a little warm. It's warmer than her skin, like a stone that has been sitting in a patch of sun. She doesn't feel it.

Across the hall the floorboards creak once as somebody in the room with the broken bolt rolls over. In the loft beyond, a carter's snoring catches, stops for a breath and starts again. No feet sound on the stairs, and nobody knocks. The bolt on her door stays shot.

*(Out of game: whatever happens next in the yard will reach her room if it gets loud enough or if someone comes up the stairs. Until then she's asleep and her rest goes on.)*

 The clatter doesn't wake Sefa. The second chance doesn't either: a little after the first noise the mule kicks its boards once more and the dog barks louder, and that dull knock through the walls doesn't reach her sleep any better than the first one did. She stays under, breathing slow and even, and the night goes on around her.
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
The noise reaches your room through a passage, a door and the house's thick old walls. Your shutter faces the Coast Way and the yard is on the far side of the house. By the time the sound gets to you it's blurred, a far-off wooden thump with an animal cry somewhere inside it.

You sleep through it. You turn onto your side and the straw tick rustles. You pull the blanket up over your shoulder without waking. The dog barking in the lane becomes part of whatever you are dreaming, and then it fades.

Across the hall the floorboards creak once as somebody in the room with the broken bolt rolls over. In the loft beyond, a carter's snoring catches, stops for a breath and starts again. No feet sound on the stairs, and nobody knocks. The bolt on your door stays shot.

A little after the first noise the mule kicks its boards once more and the dog barks louder. That dull knock through the walls doesn't reach your sleep any better than the first one did. You stay under, breathing slow and even, and the night goes on around you.

*(Out of game: whatever happens next in the yard will reach your room if it gets loud enough or if someone comes up the stairs. Until then you're asleep and your rest goes on.)*
````

## Round 15

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram is up before the last log stops rolling. The shield comes onto his arm with the ease of long habit; the sword clears its sheath with a short rasp meant to be heard. He doesn't run. He steps out from the wheel into the lamplight, three paces, and plants himself between the man and the middle wagon with the lamp behind him so the intruder is looking into light and Bram is a shape with a shield.

"Stay on that knee." Not loud — he doesn't need to be, the yard's already woken itself. "Hands where I can see them, and you'll keep all your fingers. You've got a man behind you between you and the fence, so there's no going back the way you came, and there's a bar on the gate. That leaves talking."

He lets that land, then: "Red neckcloth. You're Harn. You put a knife under that lid on the Nashkel road, and you came back tonight to ask a stable boy when the wagons leave, and now you're in Vosk's yard at midnight with no lantern. So tell me what you want with that box, and tell me straight, because the Amnian's already said more truth tonight than you're likely to, and I'd like to hear you beat him."

He keeps the sword low, point toward the ground, shield up, eyes on the man's hands — and a flick of a glance to where Pell went, to be sure the dark between fence and wagon is held.

Pell Dunmore: Pell is already moving as the first log turns, low and silent and quick as a shadow. The skitter of wood gives him the cover of sound; he crosses the ten feet before the man has got his leg free.

He drops one knee into the small of the man's back and catches the knife-hand forearm at the wrist, twisting it out and down so the fingers splay on the packed earth. His other hand pushes the man's shoulder flat. The sheath at the belt he leaves untouched for now; he pins the arm and trammels the leg with his weight.

"Don't get up," he says, low and close to the man's ear, "and you'll walk out of this with the breath in you. Move for the knife and you'll bleed where you lie."

He shifts his foot to trap the man's other arm briefly as he settles, then raises his voice to the yard, loud enough for Bram and the inn: "Free of the boards, on the ground, between me and the first wagon — with a blade at his belt. Loose the mule quiet and bring the lamp."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram is on his feet before the last log stops rolling. The shield comes up onto his arm, and the longsword leaves its sheath with a short, deliberate rasp, loud enough to be heard. He doesn't run. He takes three steps out from the wheel into the lamplight and stops between the man and the middle wagon, with the lamp on its nail behind him. To the man on his knee among the logs he's a dark shape with a shield and a sword held low, and the light is in his eyes.

"Stay on that knee." Bram doesn't raise his voice. The yard has woken itself already. "Hands where I can see them, and you'll keep all your fingers."

The man's head snaps round toward the voice. His hand stops halfway to his belt.

Behind him Pell is already moving. The sliding logs cover any sound his boots make, and the man is looking into the lamp at Bram, so the ten feet between them are dark and empty. Pell drives a knee into the small of his back, reaching for the wrist on the knife side as he comes down.

Pell's knee lands, but not squarely. The man is bigger than he looked hanging off the fence, and he's still half braced on one knee. He feels the weight hit him and heaves up hard against it. Pell's fingers close on the wrist and slide off a sweaty cuff. An elbow comes back blind and catches Pell across the ribs. It's more of a shove than a blow, and it tips him sideways onto the logs he stacked himself. They roll under his hip with a hollow clack.

The man staggers to his feet and backs away from the two of them until his shoulders hit the tailboard of the third wagon. He's tall, breathing hard, and favouring the hip he landed on. In the lamplight the cloth at his throat is red after all. His right hand has found the hilt of the knife at his belt and closed on it. The blade is still in its sheath.

Bram hasn't moved from where he planted himself. When the scrambling stops, he finishes what he was saying, at the same level pitch as before.

"You've got a man behind you between you and the fence, so there's no going back the way you came, and there's a bar on the gate. That leaves talking." He lets it sit for a breath. "Red neckcloth. You're Harn. You put a knife under that lid on the Nashkel road, and you came back tonight to ask a stable boy when the wagons leave, and now you're in Vosk's yard at midnight with no lantern. So tell me what you want with that box, and tell me straight, because the Amnian's already said more truth tonight than you're likely to, and I'd like to hear you beat him."

Bram glances once toward the fence. Pell is getting up off the logs on one hand, still on the fence side of the man. The way back to the lane is still held.

Harn looks from the sword to the shape behind him and back again. His knuckles stay white on the hilt.

"Who in the hells are *you*?" His voice is thick with ale and rough from shouting in taprooms. "He's got no guards. We *were* his guards." He jerks his chin at the middle wagon without taking his eyes off Bram. "Straight, you want. Straight is he owes me two days and the second half, and he called me a thief in front of Tamsk for looking. *Looking.*" He spits to one side. "That thing sings at night. It froze the lid in summer. A man doesn't haul that for wool money, and he doesn't pay his guards wool money for walking next to it, either."

Behind the stable door, straw rustles hard. A small shape bolts out of the dark between the stalls: Pim, barefoot, with his shirt half on. He takes one look at the man by the third wagon and doesn't stop to argue. He runs for the yard door, gets it open and goes through. His feet hit the inn's stairs, and the third step creaks.

Harn hears it too. His eyes flick toward the inn door, then back to the sword. He doesn't let go of the knife.

*(Out of game: Pell's pin failed even after the reroll, so I didn't use his lines to Harn or his shout to the yard. They assumed Harn was held down. Pell's player can say those or something else next round.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) The one contest covered all of it: knee, wrist and arm. Pell's knee hit Harn's back, but Harn heaved up under him, the wrist slid out of Pell's grip, and the arm was never twisted down or pinned.

2) Pushing the shoulder flat was part of that same pin, so it fell with it. Harn was already rising and threw Pell off onto the logs before he could press him down.

3) Pell's line and his shout both described Harn already held face-down ("Don't get up", "on the ground, between me and the first wagon"). That never happened, so I left them unsaid in the story. If his player wants him to say them, or something like them, now that Harn is on his feet, that's next round.
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 15 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I remain asleep, unaware of what’s happening in the yard.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bare feet come up the stairs fast. The third step creaks, then the seventh, and someone light and out of breath runs down the passage past Sefa's door. Further along, at the far end, a fist knocks on a door three times and doesn't stop. "Master Vosk. Master *Vosk*. The tall one's come."

Sefa doesn't wake. The knocking gets into her dream as something ordinary, a cart wheel on cobbles maybe, or a sister at the ward door, and it slides off again. She sleeps on.

At the end of the passage a bolt draws back. A floorboard groans under a heavy man getting up too fast, and a low voice asks a question. The boy answers it in a rush. Then slow, heavy steps come back along the passage, and a lamp's light crosses the crack under Sefa's door and moves on. The steps go down the stairs, the third and seventh treads creak under the weight, and a moment later the boy's bare feet follow.

The passage is quiet again. Across the hall, whoever is in the room with the broken bolt turns over and mutters something. In the loft the carter is still snoring.

Under Sefa's hand the cracked bead has cooled to the warmth of her skin. Her own bolt is still shot and her breathing is slow and even. Whatever is going on downstairs, none of it has reached her yet.
````

## Round 16

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't move. He lets the knife-hand stay where it is, because a man gripping a sheathed hilt is a man who hasn't decided yet, and Bram would rather he decided slowly.

"Bram Halloway. Caravan guard. Ten years on this road, which is longer than you've been drinking at the Red Sheaf, so don't tell me what a man hauls for wool money." It comes out level, almost tired. "And I'll give you this much: if Vosk owes you two days and a half-share, that's a fair grievance, and I've walked off runs for less. But a man who's owed money comes to the front door in daylight and says so where Orla can hear it. He doesn't come over a fence at midnight with his hand on a knife. So we've got your grievance. Now give me the other thing — the thing that made you climb instead of knock."

He tips his head a fraction toward the middle wagon behind him without looking at it.

"You had your blade under that band at the noon halt. Nobody does that for curiosity on a factor's run; they do it because they've heard something, or been offered something. So which was it? Did somebody in Nashkel tell you what's in there? Did somebody here tonight tell you what it's worth?" A beat. "Because if there's a buyer in this town for a Cowled seal, I'd like his name before we're four days up the Way with him behind us, and you'd like it told now, in a yard, rather than later to a Fist sergeant in the Seatower. Those are your two doors, Harn. Pick the one with Orla's bread at the end of it."

He flicks one look past Harn's shoulder. "Dunmore. You whole?" Then, back on the man's hands, and quieter: "Vosk is coming down those stairs now. Have the knife-hand empty by the time he gets here. It'll go easier for your two days."

Pell Dunmore: Pell pushes himself up off the tumbled logs with a grunt, and instead of closing again he rises only as far as a crouch, weight on the balls of his feet. The red neckcloth and the white knuckles are plain in the lamplight. He's still between Harn and the fence, but now he makes more room, not less — one careful step sideways, hands out and empty, so the man can see he isn't reaching.

"That's the difference between tonight and the last time he called you a thief," Pell says, pitched to cut through Harn's ragged breathing. "Last time, you walked away with no sword at your neck. This yard's got three of us, the gate's barred, and the boy's gone to wake the inn." He tips his head, not unkindly. "Drunk men die in yards like this for less. Put the knife on the ground and talk to us with both hands still yours."

He holds his ground, watching Harn's weight and not just his hands — ready to move if the man bolts or drags the blade free — and lets Bram's steel do its own quiet work behind him.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram doesn't step forward and doesn't raise the sword. He talks to Harn's hands, level and close to tired, and lets each sentence land before the next one: his name, his ten years, the grievance he'll allow and the climb he won't. Then the noon halt, and the two doors, the yard or the Seatower.

Behind Harn, Pell gets up off the logs with a grunt, but only into a crouch. He takes one careful step sideways with his empty hands held out where the man can see them. He's still between Harn and the fence, but he has left Harn room to breathe. He says his piece over Harn's ragged breathing: three of them in the yard, the gate barred, the boy gone to wake the inn, and drunk men dying in yards like this one for less. *Put the knife on the ground and talk to us with both hands still yours.*

Harn's eyes go from the shield to Pell and back. The yard door's latch rattles as someone on the other side fumbles at it.

Harn breathes out long and unsteady. Then his fingers open.

He doesn't draw the knife. He works it free of his belt, sheath and all, holding it by the leather with two fingers like something hot. He drops it on the packed earth in front of his boots and pushes it a foot away with his toe, toward Bram. It's an old guard's bargain: *you can see it, and I can't reach it.* Then he leans back against the third wagon's tailboard and takes the weight off his bad hip.

"Halloway." He says it sourly, as if it tastes of something. "Fine."

He looks past Bram at the middle wagon.

"Nobody told me nothing in Nashkel. Nobody offered me nothing. I loaded it." He jerks his chin. "Back of the forfeit house, off the square, before sunup, Vosk and a clerk in grey and nobody else about. You don't load cloth before sunup out a back door. And that wax. Every carter in Nashkel knows that wax. The hand and the coin, that's what the Amnians put on what they take off you at the border." He swallows. "So I knew it was took. I didn't know what."

Then what Bram said a moment ago catches up with him, and his face changes in the lamplight.

"Cowled." It comes out quieter than anything he's said yet. "*That's* what it says on there? Gods' teeth." He wipes his mouth with the back of his hand. "I can't read their scratching. I thought it was customs. Some lord's silver plate the excise took."

Bram gets the rest of it the way he's heard it from a dozen men leaning on a dozen wagons: sullen, in pieces, and only as much as the man can stand to say.

"I wasn't going to take it. I was going to *look*. That's all. Look, and then go to him." He tips his head at the inn. "Say I know what you're carrying, master, and I know who took it off who, so you pay me my two days and the second half and a bit for my trouble, or I go and tell the Fist sergeant at Beregost gate what's in your linen." He spits to one side. "Fair price for keeping my mouth shut. Fairer than he paid for my feet."

He shifts on the bad hip and winces.

"Tamsk said leave it. Said the mules wouldn't stand by that wagon the night the frost came, and when mules won't stand by a thing you leave it alone. He went home." He looks at Pell behind him, then at Bram's sword. "I drank till I didn't agree with him. That's the whole of it. Nobody in this town knows it's here but me. I didn't tell nobody at the Sheaf. I'm not *that* drunk."

The yard door opens.

Vosk stands in the gap in his shirt and breeches with the brown coat thrown over them, holding a candle-lamp. His feet are bare in his boots and the laces are untied. Pim hangs at his elbow. Vosk takes in the yard: the scattered logs, the knife on the ground, the man with the red neckcloth against his third wagon, and Bram's sword. He was on the stairs for the last of it. Whether he heard the words *Fist sergeant* or only the voice, his face doesn't say.

"Harn," he says. He doesn't come any closer. He speaks to Bram, low and exact, as if he's correcting a column in the ledger. "He was paid to Beregost, Master Halloway. Every day to the day, in Nashkel silver, in front of Tamsk. The second half was for the Gate, and he did not walk to the Gate." His hand is steady on the lamp. "I owe him nothing. I would like that said before anything else is."

Harn's jaw works, and he says nothing yet. In the stable the end-stall mule has gone quiet. Out in the lane the dog has given up barking.
````

Rolls: Bram Halloway Intimidation (+3): get Harn to let go of the knife and say why he climbed instead of knocking 18 vs 10 success

````text
Past Harn, Bram Halloway doesn't move. His sword stays where it is and so does Harn's knife-hand. He talks to the man level, almost tired:

"Bram Halloway. Caravan guard. Ten years on this road, which is longer than you've been drinking at the Red Sheaf, so don't tell me what a man hauls for wool money. And I'll give you this much: if Vosk owes you two days and a half-share, that's a fair grievance, and I've walked off runs for less. But a man who's owed money comes to the front door in daylight and says so where Orla can hear it. He doesn't come over a fence at midnight with his hand on a knife. So we've got your grievance. Now give me the other thing — the thing that made you climb instead of knock."

He tips his head a fraction toward the middle wagon behind him without looking at it.

"You had your blade under that band at the noon halt. Nobody does that for curiosity on a factor's run; they do it because they've heard something, or been offered something. So which was it? Did somebody in Nashkel tell you what's in there? Did somebody here tonight tell you what it's worth?" A beat. "Because if there's a buyer in this town for a Cowled seal, I'd like his name before we're four days up the Way with him behind us, and you'd like it told now, in a yard, rather than later to a Fist sergeant in the Seatower. Those are your two doors, Harn. Pick the one with Orla's bread at the end of it."

His eyes flick past Harn's shoulder to you. "Dunmore. You whole?" Then back on the man's hands, and quieter: "Vosk is coming down those stairs now. Have the knife-hand empty by the time he gets here. It'll go easier for your two days."

Bram doesn't step forward and doesn't raise the sword. He lets each sentence land before the next one.

You get up off the logs with a grunt, but only into a crouch, and take one careful step sideways with your empty hands held out where the man can see them. You're still between Harn and the fence, but you've left him room to breathe, and you say your piece over his ragged breathing.

Harn's eyes go from Bram's shield to you and back. The yard door's latch rattles as someone on the other side fumbles at it.

Harn breathes out long and unsteady. Then his fingers open.

He doesn't draw the knife. He works it free of his belt, sheath and all, holding it by the leather with two fingers like something hot. He drops it on the packed earth in front of his boots and pushes it a foot away with his toe, toward Bram. It's an old guard's bargain: *you can see it, and I can't reach it.* Then he leans back against the third wagon's tailboard and takes the weight off his bad hip.

"Halloway." He says it sourly, as if it tastes of something. "Fine."

He looks past Bram at the middle wagon.

"Nobody told me nothing in Nashkel. Nobody offered me nothing. I loaded it." He jerks his chin. "Back of the forfeit house, off the square, before sunup, Vosk and a clerk in grey and nobody else about. You don't load cloth before sunup out a back door. And that wax. Every carter in Nashkel knows that wax. The hand and the coin, that's what the Amnians put on what they take off you at the border." He swallows. "So I knew it was took. I didn't know what."

Then what Bram said a moment ago catches up with him, and his face changes in the lamplight.

"Cowled." It comes out quieter than anything he's said yet. "*That's* what it says on there? Gods' teeth." He wipes his mouth with the back of his hand. "I can't read their scratching. I thought it was customs. Some lord's silver plate the excise took."

The rest comes sullen, in pieces, and only as much as the man can stand to say.

"I wasn't going to take it. I was going to *look*. That's all. Look, and then go to him." He tips his head at the inn. "Say I know what you're carrying, master, and I know who took it off who, so you pay me my two days and the second half and a bit for my trouble, or I go and tell the Fist sergeant at Beregost gate what's in your linen." He spits to one side. "Fair price for keeping my mouth shut. Fairer than he paid for my feet."

He shifts on the bad hip and winces.

"Tamsk said leave it. Said the mules wouldn't stand by that wagon the night the frost came, and when mules won't stand by a thing you leave it alone. He went home." He looks at you behind him, then at Bram's sword. "I drank till I didn't agree with him. That's the whole of it. Nobody in this town knows it's here but me. I didn't tell nobody at the Sheaf. I'm not *that* drunk."

The yard door opens.

Vosk stands in the gap in his shirt and breeches with the brown coat thrown over them, holding a candle-lamp. His feet are bare in his boots and the laces are untied. Pim hangs at his elbow. Vosk takes in the yard: the scattered logs, the knife on the ground, the man with the red neckcloth against his third wagon, and Bram's sword. He was on the stairs for the last of it. Whether he heard the words *Fist sergeant* or only the voice, his face doesn't say.

"Harn," he says. He doesn't come any closer. He speaks to Bram, low and exact, as if he's correcting a column in the ledger. "He was paid to Beregost, Master Halloway. Every day to the day, in Nashkel silver, in front of Tamsk. The second half was for the Gate, and he did not walk to the Gate." His hand is steady on the lamp. "I owe him nothing. I would like that said before anything else is."

Harn's jaw works, and he says nothing yet. In the stable the end-stall mule has gone quiet. Out in the lane the dog has given up barking.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 16 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I sleep on until something wakes me or morning comes.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa sleeps on.

The house has gone still again since the heavy steps went down. The voices in the yard are on the far side of the building, and they stay low, nobody shouting. What little gets up through the floorboards is a murmur, too faint to make out words or tell one man from another. In the loft the carter is snoring again, steady as before.

Out on the Coast Way the cold has settled hard. The shutter creaks once as the frame cools. A cart axle somewhere down the road gives a single slow groan, probably a wagon settling where it stands, and then the street goes quiet. Nobody passes under her window.

Sefa turns onto her back under the two blankets. The beads have slid off her palm onto the tick beside her. Her breathing is slow and even. The bolt is still shot, and nothing comes up the stairs.

*(Out of game: it's still deep night, and the yard business is going on at the same hour. Sefa's sleep carries on until that scene either reaches her door or runs on to first light.)*
````
