## Round 17

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 17 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram lets his sword arm drop until the point rests on the packed earth. He doesn't sheathe it. He steps forward, puts his boot on Harn's knife and drags it back to his own feet, then stoops and picks it up by the sheath without taking his eyes off the man.

"Heard, Master Vosk. Paid to Beregost, in front of a witness. That's the ledger closed." He says it like he's initialling it himself. "Now the other column."

He turns the sheathed knife over once in his hand and looks at Harn.

"You were going to put a price on your silence. You've just given the whole of it away to three strangers and the man you meant to squeeze, in his own yard, with the stable boy listening. So that trade's dead. What you've got left is this: nobody's bleeding, and I'd like to keep it so." He tosses the knife underhand, not to Harn but onto the straw a few paces to his side, where it takes a step and a stoop to reach. "That's yours. You pick it up when you're on the far side of that gate and not before. Pim will lift the bar. You go up the street, you sleep it off, and in the morning you take the south road the way Tamsk did. If I see that neckcloth between here and the Gate, I'll take it for an answer to a question I didn't ask, and I'll stop asking questions."

A beat, and then, more quietly, almost man to man:

"Tamsk was right about the mules. I'd have listened to him."

Then he turns his head to Vosk, and his voice goes back to the level it had at the hearth table.

"Two things before anyone goes back to bed, and I want them straight. First — your bill of release. Is that paper good at a Fist gate, or isn't it? Because if a sergeant at the Beregost road-post can look at it and wave us through, then every threat this man made is wind, and I'll sleep. If it's the kind of paper that makes a sergeant go and fetch his captain, then we've got a problem at the first post we come to, and I'd rather know it tonight." He nods at Harn. "He loaded it out a back door before sunup with a clerk in grey. That's not how honest wool moves, and you know it."

"Second — he says the mules wouldn't stand by that wagon the night the frost came. You didn't mention that in the common room. I told you the terms: a horse won't go near it, we stop and talk. Looks like we've already had that night and I wasn't there for it." He lets the sword rest. "So talk. Short. Then Pim opens the gate, this man leaves, and I go back to my wheel."

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays in his crouch at the edge of the lamplight, hands still open and resting on his knees. He watches Harn through Vosk's quiet statement, then glances once at the knife in the dirt. No one else moves for it.

"You're owed a reckoning, not a wage," Pell says, low, to Harn. "And now the reckoning's here, with your knife in the dirt and your words already spent. You came over the fence with a plan to squeeze the man. In the dark. With your hand on steel." He lets that land. "You tell me what you'd call that, if you were the one with the lamp and the ledger."

He straightens, slowly, not crowding, and looks toward Vosk. His voice carries just enough. "He says he followed the box from Nashkel and meant to look first and sell the secret after. That's his whole account, unless he finds a better one." Then his eyes go back to Harn, and there's no heat in it, just a settled kind of attention. "So you picked the right door, Harn — the one with Orla's bread at the end of it. If you keep your arse on that tailboard and your hands where they are, you might still get it."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram lowers the sword until its point rests on the packed earth. He keeps it out. He takes one step, sets his boot on Harn's knife and drags it back through the straw to his own feet. Then he stoops, picks it up by the sheath and straightens, and his eyes never leave Harn while he does it.

"Heard, Master Vosk. Paid to Beregost, in front of a witness. That's the ledger closed." He says it the way a man initials a page. "Now the other column."

He tells Harn the trade is dead, because he gave his secret away for nothing to three strangers, the man he meant to squeeze and a stable boy. What's left is that nobody is bleeding. He tosses the knife underhand onto the straw a few paces to Harn's side, close enough to reach with a step and a stoop. He sets the terms: the knife gets picked up on the far side of the gate, Pim lifts the bar, Harn sleeps it off up the street and takes the south road in the morning. If Bram sees that neckcloth anywhere between here and the Gate, he stops asking questions.

Then, quieter: "Tamsk was right about the mules. I'd have listened to him."

Harn looks at the knife lying in the straw and doesn't move for it. His jaw works.

"South road." His voice comes out thick. "Fine." He shifts his weight on the tailboard, and the bad hip makes him wince. "Tamsk's always right. That's what's wrong with him."

Pell stays in his crouch at the edge of the light with his open hands resting on his knees. He talks to Harn low and without heat: Harn is owed a reckoning, not a wage, and the reckoning has come. He came over a fence in the dark with a plan to squeeze a man and his hand on steel. *You tell me what you'd call that, if you were the one with the lamp and the ledger.* Then Pell straightens slowly, taking care not to crowd him, and speaks toward the door. "He says he followed the box from Nashkel and meant to look first and sell the secret after. That's his whole account, unless he finds a better one."

"Didn't *follow* it." Harn's head comes up. "Loaded it. I told him." He jerks his chin at Bram.

Pell lets that go by. He looks back at Harn with the same settled attention as before. *So you picked the right door. Keep your arse on that tailboard and your hands where they are, and you might still get your bread.*

Harn stays on the tailboard. He folds his arms, which keeps his hands where everyone can see them. He doesn't seem to notice he's done it.

Then Bram turns his head toward the yard door and puts his two questions at the hearth-table pitch: whether the bill of release is good at a gate post, and why nobody mentioned the night the mules wouldn't stand.

Vosk has stood in the doorway through all of it with the candle-lamp held steady. He keeps quiet until Bram has finished, then steps down into the yard. Pim stays behind on the threshold.

"The paper first." He keeps his voice low, though there's no one in the yard he could hide it from now. "It is good in Amn. An officer at the border post above Nashkel read it and waved me through. On this side of the border, Master Halloway, nobody licenses magic. A man in the Gate may own what the Cowled would hang him for in Athkatla. A sergeant at a road post can't read Amnian, most of them. He sees a stamp, a seal and a merchant. If he wants more than that, what he wants is silver, and I carry silver for that. If he fetches his captain, the captain finds no law of the Gate broken." His eyes go to Harn for the first time and come away again. "What he could have sold the Fist was never a crime. It was a story. *Something that frightens Amnian wizards is on the road north, in a cloth wagon, with two guards short.* A story like that doesn't bring a sergeant. It brings a different sort of man altogether."

He pauses.

"The back door. The clerk told me forfeit goods are not carried out across the square, where the man they were taken from might be standing and watching them go. That is what he said. It sounded sensible to me, and I took it. You may think what you like of it. I have had four days on the road to think the same."

Then he turns to the middle wagon.

"The mules. Yes. It was the night before Beregost, the night the frost came. The middle team pulled their pickets and stood at the far end of the line until it was light. In the morning they went into the shafts the same as always and pulled all day without trouble." He says it plainly and doesn't look away. "I did not tell you at the table. You did not ask, and I didn't want to lose you before you had said yes. That was wrong of me, and I'll not pretend otherwise."

"But I told you I would argue, and here it is. You made your terms after that night. Tonight those same mules have stood in a stable with that wagon on the other side of the boards, and they never stirred until the logs came down." He lifts the lamp a little toward the stalls. In the end stall the mule has its head over the half-door, ears up, looking at the scattered logs and not at the wagon. "So we are stopped, and we have talked. Short, as you asked. Whether it is enough is for you to say, Master Halloway."

Pim stands on the threshold behind him with one hand on the doorframe, and his eyes go between Bram and the barred gate. The bar is still down in its brackets. Harn sits on the tailboard with his arms folded and waits to be let out.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) No roll was needed. Taking a dropped knife off the ground and stating terms had no chance of going wrong, and the turn shows it: the point of the sword rests on the ground with the blade still out, the knife is on the straw beside Harn, and the terms are given.

2) No roll was needed, since it's a question to Vosk. He answered in the scene: the paper is good in Amn, nobody licenses magic on this side of the border, and the danger is the story getting out, not the law.

3) No roll was needed here either. Vosk answered in the scene: the middle team pulled their pickets the night of the frost, he kept quiet about it so he wouldn't lose Bram, and he argues that tonight the mules stood quiet. The turn stops there because he ended by asking whether that's enough, and that's Bram's to answer.
````

````text
Sefa sleeps on.

Nothing new reaches the room. Down in the yard, on the far side of the house, the talk has gone on low and steady. Through the walls it comes to her room as a murmur, no louder than wind in a chimney, and she can't make out a single word. No boots come up the stairs. Across the hall the man in the room with the broken bolt has gone quiet, and in the loft the carter's snoring has slowed into long, rattling breaths. **Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**
````

````text
You sleep on.

Nothing new reaches the room. Down in the yard, on the far side of the house, the talk has gone on low and steady. Through the walls it comes to your room as a murmur, no louder than wind in a chimney, and you can't make out a single word. No boots come up the stairs. The lamp that crossed under your door a little while ago hasn't come back.

The cold keeps settling. Somewhere in the wall beside the washstand a beam ticks once as it shrinks. Across the hall the man in the room with the broken bolt has gone quiet, and in the loft the carter's snoring has slowed into long, rattling breaths. Out on the Coast Way a cat goes along the eaves of the house next door. Its claws scratch on a loose shingle and then it's gone.

You lie on your side with one arm out of the blanket and your hand on the tick near the beads. Your breathing hasn't changed. The bolt is still shot, and the cracks in the shutter are still black. Dawn is hours away.

*(Out of game: your night carries on alongside the yard. If nothing down there comes up to your door, you sleep through until the shutter turns grey.)*
````

## Round 18

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

*Bram Halloway pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram listens to the whole of it with the sword point in the dirt, and when Vosk is done he's quiet for the length of a breath.

"It's enough." He slides the longsword home. "Not because I like the answer. Because you gave it without me having to dig, and because the mule's looking at the firewood and not the wagon. That's the test I set, and it's passed, tonight. We'll set it again every night from here to the Gate, and I'll be awake for the next one." A short nod toward the stalls. "But hear this, Master Vosk, and take it for the last time I say it kindly: on the road, you don't wait to be asked. Mules pull a picket, frost on a lid, a clerk in grey — you tell me when it happens, not when I catch you at it. I can't stand in the right place for a thing I've not been told about. You held back once to keep me. Hold back twice and you'll lose me, and you'll lose me at the worst mile on the Way."

Then he turns to Pim on the threshold.

"Lift the bar, lad. One leaf, wide enough for a man." He looks at Harn. "Up. Slow. Knife when you're through, not before — I'll kick it out after you." He waits until Harn's moved off the tailboard before he adds, "And Harn. The Amnian said it better than I did. What you knew was never worth anything to a sergeant. It was worth something to the kind of man who doesn't wear a badge, and you'd have gone looking for him sober. Be glad you were drunk." He watches the man through the gap, kicks the sheathed knife after him into the lane, and tells Pim: "Bar it. Then bed."

To Pell, once the bar's down: "You did well. Knee was a hair high, but the logs did the rest, and nobody bled." He picks the scattered split oak up two at a time and leans it back under the low board, the same careless balance as before. "Your watch still. Wake me at grey."

And to Vosk, as the factor turns for the door, quieter: "Go back to bed. Whatever's in that box didn't stir for any of this. I'm starting to think it's not the box we need to watch on this road. It's who's heard of it."

He settles back against the wheel, shield across his lap, and closes his eyes.

 — to first light, unless the yard objects.

Pell Dunmore: Pell keeps his place between Harn and the fence, letting Bram and Vosk talk it out. He listens with the same close quiet he's held all night, turning Vosk's words over for the seams.

When Vosk finishes, Pell glances toward the stable, where the mule's ears are up and its eyes are on the logs, not the wagon. Then he looks back at Bram. "I've got this side," he says, low. "Say the word and the gate opens." He doesn't move for the bar, and he doesn't take his eyes off Harn, but the offer's plain. His own back is to the dark lane, the woodpile's spilled logs at his heels, and he seems in no hurry to leave the yard.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
"It's enough."

Bram slides the longsword home. Vosk watches the blade go into the scabbard and lets out a breath he has plainly been holding since the stairs. He hears the rest out with the candle-lamp steady: the test set again every night from here to the Gate, and the warning that on the road nobody waits to be asked. Pulled pickets, frost on the lid, a clerk in grey: Bram is to be told when it happens, not when he catches it. He held back once to keep Bram on. Do it twice, and he loses Bram at the worst mile on the Way.

Vosk doesn't argue this time.

"Understood, Master Halloway. When it happens, then. Not when you ask." He says it slowly, like a man writing a clause into a contract he means to keep. "You'll have it at the hour it happens, whatever the hour is."

Bram turns toward the threshold. Before he gets the words out, Pell speaks up from the fence side, low, his eyes still on Harn. "I've got this side. Say the word and the gate opens." He doesn't move toward the bar.

Bram gives the word to Pim. *Lift the bar, lad. One leaf, wide enough for a man.*

"One leaf, wide enough for a man." Pim is across the yard before he finishes repeating it. He has to get his shoulder under the bar to shift it out of the brackets, and he lays it carefully on the ground so it won't bang. Then he drags one leaf of the gate inward, and its bottom edge scrapes across the packed earth. Past the gap the Coast Way is grey and empty, and every door along it is dark.

*Up. Slow.*

Harn pushes himself off the tailboard. The bad hip catches him on the first step, and he walks the rest of the way to the gate stiffly, giving Bram a wide berth. He doesn't look at the middle wagon. Pell turns where he stands to keep him in view and lets him pass.

At the gap Bram tells him the rest: what he knew was never worth anything to a sergeant. It was worth something to the kind of man who doesn't wear a badge, and he'd have gone looking for that man sober.

Harn stops with one boot in the lane and looks back. His face is hard to read in the lamplight. It might be anger. It might be the drink catching up with him all at once.

"Didn't need telling twice," he says. It's not clear whether he means Bram or the Amnian.

Bram puts his boot to the sheathed knife and sends it skidding across the straw and out through the gap. It comes to rest against the far side of the lane. Harn stoops for it, slowly, one hand on his hip. He shoves it into his belt and limps away up the Coast Way, north, toward the dark bulk of the Red Sheaf. He doesn't look back again.

"Bar it. Then bed."

Pim swings the leaf shut and gets his shoulder under the bar again. It drops into the brackets with a thump. "Barred, master." Then he trots back to the stable without waiting to be told twice. Straw rustles, and the boy goes quiet.

Bram tells Pell he did well. The knee was a hair high, the logs did the rest, and nobody bled. Then he picks the logs up off the ground two at a time and leans them back under the low board, propped against each other the same careless way, so they'll fall if anything touches them. *Your watch still. Wake me at grey.*

Vosk has turned for the door. Bram's last word reaches him there. *It's not the box we need to watch on this road. It's who's heard of it.*

Vosk stands with his hand on the latch for a moment.

"I've thought the same since Nashkel, Master Halloway," he says, without turning round. "It is a poor thing to find out you are right." Then he goes in, and his light moves away through the passage and up the stairs.

Bram works himself back down against the wheel of the middle wagon, shield across his lap, and shuts his eyes. Within a few minutes his breathing has gone light and even again.

---

The yard has gone back to its night noises. The lamp on its nail by the door burns lower. The end-stall mule watches the logs a while longer, gives up and drops its head. The hum comes up faint and steady through the wagon boards, the same as it was before midnight and the same as when the logs came down. Nothing has changed in it. Pell has the yard to himself, the barred gate, the leaning logs and the empty lane behind the fence. Nobody else comes down the lane.

The cold settles hardest in the last hour, and then the sky above the stable roof starts going from black to the colour of slate. Somewhere up the Coast Way a cock crows too early and gives it up. Farther off, the first forge of the day clangs twice as someone knocks the night's ash out of it.

The stable door creaks and Pim comes out yawning with a bucket. The scrape of the bucket handle wakes Bram the way the wrong sound always does, and he's awake before the boy reaches the trough. The mules shift and blow at the smell of water.

Then the yard door opens. Vosk stands in it fully dressed, the brown coat buttoned and his boots laced, with the ledger under his arm and his face grey from the short night. He looks along the row of wagons to the middle one, then at Bram by the wheel. Then his eyes settle on Pell.

It isn't quite light yet. He doesn't say anything. He waits.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) No roll needed: leaning six logs back against a fence is routine work for Bram, and the story shows it done.
2) No roll needed: Bram gave Pim the order, and Pim lifted the bar and later dropped it back, as shown.
3) No roll needed: Bram kicked a sheathed knife a few feet through an open gate, unopposed. It went out into the lane and Harn picked it up.
4) No roll needed: Bram was telling Harn the truth, and Harn answered him ("Didn't need telling twice") before he limped off.
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 18 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I continue sleeping until dawn or until something wakes me.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa sleeps through the last of the night without waking.

Nothing comes up the stairs. The murmur from the yard on the far side of the house dies away, and after it there's only the cold. Some time before grey a door at the end of the passage opens, and slow, heavy steps come past her room. This time no lamp crosses the crack under her door. The steps go down the stairs, the third tread creaks, then the seventh, and they're gone. She doesn't stir.

What wakes her is the street. Up the Coast Way a cock crows too early and gives up halfway through. Farther off, iron rings twice as someone knocks the night's ash out of a forge. When she opens her eyes, the cracks around the shutter have gone from black to the colour of slate.

The room is very cold. The jug on the washstand has a skin of chill on it, and her breath shows faintly above the blanket. The beads have slid down onto the tick beside her pillow, and the cracked one is as cool as the rest. The book is where she left it, and so are her clothes. The bolt is still shot.

The house is waking around her. In the loft a carter coughs, swears, and somebody's boots hit the floor. Below, a bar scrapes out of its brackets, and Orla's voice says something short to nobody in particular. Pots knock together in the kitchen. Faintly, from the yard side, comes the creak and splash of a bucket at the trough and a mule blowing at the smell of water.

She got a full night's sleep, the whole stretch from the lamps to grey, and she's rested for it.

It isn't full light yet. Vosk said he'd leave at first light, and that's close now. Nothing else happens in Sefa's room unless she makes it happen.
````

## Round 19

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 19 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram gets up off the wheel the way he got up off a hundred: slow, knees first, one hand on the spoke. He rolls his shoulders until the mail sits right, then before he says a word to anyone he climbs onto the middle wagon's bench, unties the two front lashings and folds the canvas back a hand's width.

He looks at the lid. He puts his palm flat on it, then on the linen bolt beside it. Whatever he finds, he says it aloud, plain, to Vosk in the doorway: dry or wet, cold or not, frost or none. Then he ties the lashings again and climbs down.

"Night's report," he says. "One man over the fence, sent off up the street. The box did nothing a box shouldn't. Mules slept." He nods at the end stall. "That's the first of four. Three more to go."

He turns to Pell, and doesn't speak for him.

"He's waiting on you, Dunmore. Not me. Say it or don't, but say it before the mules are in the shafts, because once they are I stop counting heads." Then, to Vosk: "Half when we start, you said. I'll take mine before the first wheel turns, and I'll want bread with it — Orla's, not hardtack. And the healer upstairs doesn't know we're leaving unless someone's told her. I'll knock on my way to the privy. She turned your coin down, that's her right, but I'd not walk north without saying so to her face. Ten minutes, and I'll be at the lead team's head."

He slings the shield onto his back for the moment, picks up the guttered candle-lamp from the dirt and hands it to Vosk in passing.

"Yours. It did its job."

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays where he has been, back against the cold stable wall, the night's work settled into his shoulders but his eyes still clear. He watches Vosk stand in the doorway and lets the quiet run a moment longer before he speaks.

"Quiet since the gate was barred." He nods once toward the fence, where the logs lean untouched. "Nobody came back for a second try."

He shifts off the wall and stands at his ease, wincing only slightly as he rolls one shoulder where Harn elbowed him. "You're abroad before the crowing," he says to Vosk. "First light's not far. I'll wash the yard off my hands and be down to give you my word."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram gets up off the wheel slowly, knees first, one hand on a spoke. The cold has settled into the mail overnight, and he rolls his shoulders until it hangs right. Then he climbs onto the middle wagon's bench, where Vosk sat yesterday, works the two front lashings loose and folds the canvas back a hand's width.

The grey light reaches the crate. There is no frost on it. He lays his palm flat on the lid, and the oak is cold. It is colder than the bench under his knee and colder than the iron band an inch from his fingers, as if the wood had spent the night somewhere else. The hum comes up into his hand the same as it did last night, no stronger and no weaker. He moves his hand to the bolt of linen pressed against the crate's far side. It is wet now, not just damp, and the wet has spread a finger's width further into the cloth than it was by lamplight.

He tells Vosk exactly that across the yard: no frost, the lid cold, the linen wetter than last night, and the hum unchanged. Then he ties the lashings off again and climbs down.

Vosk hears it from the doorway, and his mouth goes tight. "Wetter," he says, as if he were writing it in a margin. "I'll have that bolt moved to the tail of the first wagon before we yoke. It is spoiled for selling as it is." He doesn't argue the rest.

*Night's report.* Bram gives it in four short pieces: one man over the fence and sent off up the street, the box doing nothing a box shouldn't, the mules asleep, and one night down with three to go.

Pell comes off the stable wall as Bram finishes. He rolls the shoulder Harn's elbow found and winces a little.

"Quiet since the gate was barred." He nods toward the fence, where the six logs still lean against each other. "Nobody came back for a second try." Then, to Vosk: "You're abroad before the crowing. First light's not far. I'll wash the yard off my hands and be down to give you my word."

Vosk looks at him for a moment, then at the sky over the stable roof. It is slate, going pale at the eastern edge.

"Wash, then, Master Dunmore. The mules go into the shafts when I can read the ledger without a lamp." He doesn't say *and not after*, but it is plain in how he says it.

Bram turns to Pell and leaves the choice with him. He's waiting on you, not me. Say it before the mules are in the shafts, because after that Bram stops counting heads. Then he turns to Vosk: half when we start, his before the first wheel turns, and bread from Orla's kitchen, not hardtack.

Vosk opens the ledger against his forearm, takes a small purse from inside his coat and counts four gold pieces into Bram's hand without hurrying.

"Four days at two, the half of it is four. Paid." He marks the page with his pen, and this time the nib is wet. "The bread is part of your food, Master Halloway. That is mine to buy, not yours. I'll have Mistress Tarnwick put up a loaf for each man and a round of cheese. She'll be glad of the custom before breakfast."

Bram also tells him that the healer upstairs doesn't know they're leaving unless somebody tells her, and that he'll knock on her door himself. She turned the coin down, which is her right, but he won't walk north without saying it to her face. Vosk nods slowly.

"That is decent of you. She was decent to me." He closes the ledger. "Second door on the left. You know it."

Bram slings the shield onto his back, picks Vosk's candle-lamp up out of the straw and puts it in the factor's hand as he goes past. *Yours. It did its job.*

Vosk looks down at the cold glass with its burnt-out thread of tallow, and for a moment something close to a smile shows under the grey beard. "So it did."

At the trough Pim has stopped pouring and is listening with his mouth half open. Vosk raises his voice to him. "Pim. The middle team first, then the lead. Harness, not yoke. Not until I say."

"Middle team first, then the lead. Harness, not yoke." The boy says it into the bucket and goes into the stable at a trot.

Bram goes in through the yard door. The common room smells of new smoke and bread. Orla is at the hearth with a poker and doesn't look round, and the stairs are ahead of him.

*(Out of game: Bram's knock on Sefa's door happens in Sefa's scene, so her answer comes from her player there.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) I didn't roll for the crate: putting a hand on a lid and a bolt of cloth and saying what you feel can't go wrong, so the story gave the result straight: no frost, the lid cold, the linen wetter, the hum the same.

2) I didn't roll for the knock either. Bram is on his way to Sefa's door now, and since she's in her own scene, the knock and whatever she says back belong to her scene.
````

**Bram Halloway's screen (the relay):**

````text
The man who kept the other half of the watch with you, Pell Dunmore, stays with his back against the cold stable wall while you get up. He watches Vosk in the doorway and lets the quiet run a while longer.

You get up off the wheel slowly, knees first, one hand on a spoke. The cold has settled into the mail overnight, and you roll your shoulders until it hangs right. Then you climb onto the middle wagon's bench, where Vosk sat yesterday, work the two front lashings loose and fold the canvas back a hand's width.

The grey light reaches the crate. There is no frost on it. You lay your palm flat on the lid, and the oak is cold. It is colder than the bench under your knee and colder than the iron band an inch from your fingers, as if the wood had spent the night somewhere else. The hum comes up into your hand the same as it did last night, no stronger and no weaker. You move your hand to the bolt of linen pressed against the crate's far side. It is wet now, not just damp, and the wet has spread a finger's width further into the cloth than it was by lamplight.

You tell Vosk exactly that across the yard: no frost, the lid cold, the linen wetter than last night, and the hum unchanged. Then you tie the lashings off again and climb down.

Vosk hears it from the doorway, and his mouth goes tight. "Wetter," he says, as if he were writing it in a margin. "I'll have that bolt moved to the tail of the first wagon before we yoke. It is spoiled for selling as it is." He doesn't argue the rest.

You give him the night's report in four short pieces: one man over the fence and sent off up the street, the box doing nothing a box shouldn't, the mules asleep, and one night down with three to go.

Pell comes off the stable wall as you finish. He rolls the shoulder Harn's elbow found and winces a little.

"Quiet since the gate was barred." He nods toward the fence, where the six logs still lean against each other. "Nobody came back for a second try." Then, to Vosk: "You're abroad before the crowing. First light's not far. I'll wash the yard off my hands and be down to give you my word."

Vosk looks at him for a moment, then at the sky over the stable roof. It is slate, going pale at the eastern edge.

"Wash, then, Master Dunmore. The mules go into the shafts when I can read the ledger without a lamp." He doesn't say *and not after*, but it is plain in how he says it.

You turn to Pell and leave the choice with him, then turn to Vosk about your half and the bread.

Vosk opens the ledger against his forearm, takes a small purse from inside his coat and counts four gold pieces into your hand without hurrying.

"Four days at two, the half of it is four. Paid." He marks the page with his pen, and this time the nib is wet. "The bread is part of your food, Master Halloway. That is mine to buy, not yours. I'll have Mistress Tarnwick put up a loaf for each man and a round of cheese. She'll be glad of the custom before breakfast."

When you tell him about the healer upstairs, Vosk nods slowly.

"That is decent of you. She was decent to me." He closes the ledger. "Second door on the left. You know it."

You sling the shield onto your back, pick Vosk's candle-lamp up out of the straw and put it in the factor's hand as you go past.

Vosk looks down at the cold glass with its burnt-out thread of tallow, and for a moment something close to a smile shows under the grey beard. "So it did."

At the trough Pim has stopped pouring and is listening with his mouth half open. Vosk raises his voice to him. "Pim. The middle team first, then the lead. Harness, not yoke. Not until I say."

"Middle team first, then the lead. Harness, not yoke." The boy says it into the bucket and goes into the stable at a trot.

You go in through the yard door. The common room smells of new smoke and bread. Orla is at the hearth with a poker and doesn't look round, and the stairs are ahead of you.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The water in the chipped jug is cold enough to sting. Sefa splashes her face and the back of her neck, and her breath clouds over the basin. She dresses fast, the way people do in a room where the shutter's cracks have only just gone grey, and buckles on the studded leather, which is stiff from the night. The beads go round her neck with the cracked one sitting where it always sits. It is cool against her collarbone. The pale-bound book goes onto her belt, warm the way its pages always are. She shoulders the pack and draws the bolt, and it slides back with the same solid knock it made going home.

The passage is grey and smells of tallow. The door across the hall stands open a crack on an empty, unmade bed. From the loft come coughing, a boot hunting for its pair and somebody swearing. Down below, pots knock together in the kitchen, and the smell of new bread is coming up the stairwell.

She's three steps from the stairhead when the third step creaks under someone coming up.

It's a heavy tread, slow, and mail chinks with it. A shape climbs out of the stairwell's dark toward the grey light at the top: a broad-shouldered man with a round shield slung on his back, coming up to her floor. He's between her and the common room, a few steps below the seventh tread.

*(Out of game: Bram was coming up the stairs to knock on her door, so the two scenes meet here on the stairs. I've stopped at that moment so Bram's player can say what he does, and the round can go on from there with both of them.)*
````

## Round 20

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram takes the stairs, lets the third and seventh steps creak because there's no helping it, and stops at the second door on the left. He knocks twice with a knuckle, not loud — the kind of knock that says *it's not trouble, but it's not nothing either*.

"Mistress Marrin. Halloway. Vosk's yoking in a quarter hour and I said I'd not go north without telling you to your face."

If she opens, he stands back from the door and keeps it short.

"Night's news, since some of it's yours. Harn came over the fence past midnight. Drunk, bad hip, hand on a knife he never drew. Dunmore and I had him on his knees before he'd got a boot down, and he talked. He loaded that crate himself at the Nashkel forfeit house, back door before sunup, clerk in grey. He couldn't read the seal — thought it was excise. He meant to look, then squeeze Vosk for silence money. I sent him up the street with his knife and a warning. Nobody bled."

"And the box. No frost this morning. But the lid's colder than the iron bands on it, and the linen beside it's wetter than it was by lamplight — spread a finger's width. The hum's the same. The mules slept through all of it, bar the logs falling." He shifts the shield on his back. "You said it's the cold that wants explaining, not the water. It's getting colder, and it's doing it in a stable with no wind. I thought you'd want that for whatever you make of it."

Then, plainly: "You turned the coin down and I'll not argue it. But you came down that road and you've nowhere to be, as far as I heard. If you're walking north anyway, paid or not, there's bread and cheese going into the first wagon and you'd be welcome to walk beside it. If you're staying, then good luck in Beregost and thank you for reading the wax. That's all. I'll be at the lead team's head."

He waits for whatever she says, then goes down: the privy, a splash from the trough, Orla's bread, and his place at the lead mule's head with the shield on his arm.

Sefa Marrin: I stop short of the stairhead, leaving room for him to come up, and try to make out his face in the grey light.

“Good morning. Bram?”
````

````text
"Good morning. Bram?"

He stops two treads short of the top so he isn't crowding her on the landing. Below him a carter comes out of the loft passage with his boots in his hand, squeezes past with a grunt and goes on down. The seventh step creaks under him.

"Mistress Marrin. Halloway." He nods at the door behind her, the one he'd been going to knock on. "Saves me a knock. Vosk's yoking in a quarter hour, and I said I'd not go north without telling you to your face."

He couldn't read the seal. Thought it was excise. He meant to look, then squeeze Vosk for silence money. I sent him up the street with his knife and a warning. Nobody bled."

From below comes the smell of new bread and the clatter of the kitchen. He shifts the shield's strap on his shoulder.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 20 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell walks to the trough, rolls up his sleeves, and washes the yard off his hands and face with a few quick splashes of icy water. He dries them on the hem of his shirt and shakes the stiffness out of his wrists.

Then he crosses to where Vosk stands with the ledger, and says it plainly: "I'm in. Two gold a day, food, half now and half at the Basilisk Gate." A short nod toward the yard and the gate. "I'll take my place where Bram can see me, and I'll keep my hands on the outside of the wagons."

He holds his palm out without any flourish. "I'll take my four now, same as him."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell rolls his sleeves back and plunges his hands into the trough. The water is cold enough to make his wrists ache, and the night's grit and old straw lift off his skin. Two quick splashes to his face clear the last of the watch out of his eyes. He wipes himself on the hem of his shirt and shakes his hands out. From inside the stable comes the clink of buckles as Pim works, and the middle team blows and shifts under the harness.

Vosk is still standing in the yard door with the ledger under his arm. The grey over the stable roof is turning pale at its eastern edge, and he watches Pell walk the whole way across the yard to him.

"I'm in. Two gold a day, food, half now and half at the Basilisk Gate." Pell tips his head toward the gate and the wagons. "I'll take my place where Bram can see me, and I'll keep my hands on the outside of the wagons." He holds out his open palm. "I'll take my four now, same as him."

For a moment Vosk only looks at the hand. Then he opens the ledger against his forearm and takes the small purse out of his coat again. He counts four gold pieces into Pell's palm one at a time, and his eyes stay on Pell's face until the last coin is down.

"Four. Paid." He turns to the page, writes a short line under Bram's and blots it with his thumb. "You are counted, Master Dunmore. Where you walk on the road, Master Halloway will say. That is his trade, and I will not set his line for him." He closes the ledger. "You stood the watch alone after he slept. Pim tells me nobody came back down the lane." It isn't thanks, quite. It's a figure he has noted and means to keep.

His glance drops to the earthenware bowl on the straw by the stable wall, scraped clean.

"And Mistress Tarnwick's bowl goes back to her kitchen before we roll. I will not leave this town owing her crockery."

He steps back over the threshold toward the smell of bread and Orla's voice. Behind Pell the stable door bangs open. Pim backs out leading the first of the middle team, harnessed but not yet in the shafts. The mule plants its feet on the packed earth and blows at the cold. It doesn't so much as look at the middle wagon.

It's still a little short of full light. Nothing else is asked of Pell before the yoking unless he wants something.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram stays on his tread with one hand on the rail and adds nothing to what he's said. After a moment he speaks again, more quietly.

"Take your time deciding, but not much of it. When Vosk can read his ledger without a lamp, the mules go in. I've seen the sky. That's a quarter hour, no more."

From below come the clatter of the kitchen and Orla's voice telling someone short and sharp where the cheese is. A carter comes back up from the yard with wet hair and squeezes past Bram on the rail side. The seventh step creaks under him, and he nods to them both without curiosity as he goes into the loft passage.

"Thank you for coming up. And I'm glad nobody bled." Sefa shifts her pack on her shoulder. "I'm staying. Walking beside the wagon without taking pay wouldn't make my doubts about it any smaller."

For a moment she looks at the straw caught in the rings of his mail.

"You kept your word last night. Keep the condition you gave him, too. If someone wakes cold, stop. Don't let him bargain you another mile before you do."

She steps back to clear the landing so he has room to turn. "I may try High Hedge by daylight. There's something worth learning here, even if I never see that crate again."

Then she holds out her hand to him across the two treads, palm open, at the height of the rail. The grey from the little window falls on it. Behind her the door of her room stands open on the cold, unmade bed.

"Safe road, Bram. If we meet in the Gate, I'd be glad to share a table again."

She waits with her hand out.
````

````text
"Thank you for coming up. And I'm glad nobody bled." Sefa shifts her pack on her shoulder. "I'm staying. Walking beside the wagon without taking pay wouldn't make my doubts about it any smaller."

For a moment she looks at the straw caught in the rings of your mail.

"You kept your word last night. Keep the condition you gave him, too. If someone wakes cold, stop. Don't let him bargain you another mile before you do."

She steps back to clear the landing so you have room to turn. "I may try High Hedge by daylight. There's something worth learning here, even if I never see that crate again."

Then she holds out her hand to you across the two treads, palm open, at the height of the rail. The grey from the little window falls on it. Behind her the door of her room stands open on the cold, unmade bed.

"Safe road, Bram. If we meet in the Gate, I'd be glad to share a table again."

She waits with her hand out.
````

**Sefa Marrin's screen (the relay):**

````text
Bram stays on his tread with one hand on the rail and adds nothing to what he's said. After a moment he speaks again, more quietly.

"Take your time deciding, but not much of it. When Vosk can read his ledger without a lamp, the mules go in. I've seen the sky. That's a quarter hour, no more."

From below come the clatter of the kitchen and Orla's voice telling someone short and sharp where the cheese is. A carter comes back up from the yard with wet hair and squeezes past Bram on the rail side. The seventh step creaks under him, and he nods to you both without curiosity as he goes into the loft passage.

You hold out your hand to Bram across the two treads, palm open, at the height of the rail. The grey from the little window falls on it. Behind you the door of your room stands open on the cold, unmade bed.

You wait with your hand out.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 21 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell closes his hand over the four gold, drops them into his purse without counting, and gives Vosk a small nod. "Counted, then."

He stoops for the bowl by the stable wall, knocks the straw dust from it, and follows a few steps behind the factor toward the door. "I'll put this back in the kitchen before we roll," he says, half to Vosk, half to the morning.

Before he goes in, he pauses in the doorway and takes one last look across the yard: the middle wagon, the mule already out and not even glancing at it, the logs still leaning under the fence. Then he slips inside, moving through the warm, bread-smelling dimness toward the kitchen to hand over Orla's bowl and see if there's hot bread, weak ale, and any word of Sefa coming down.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The four gold go into Pell's purse without a sound. "Counted, then." Vosk nods once and is already turning toward the smell of bread.

Pell picks the bowl up off the straw and knocks it against his palm to shake the dust out. "I'll put this back in the kitchen before we roll."

He stops on the threshold and looks back at the yard. The first mule of the middle team stands harnessed by the trough. It blows at the cold and keeps its head turned toward Pim and the stable, nowhere near the middle wagon. The canvas on that wagon is lashed tight again. Past the third wagon the six logs still lean against each other under the low board. Over the stable roof the slate has a pale edge to it now.

Inside, the common room is dim and warm. Someone has raked the hearth up, and the first flames are licking at new wood. The benches are empty except for a carter asleep sitting up with his cheek on his folded arms. The kitchen passage smells of yeast and smoke. Vosk is halfway down it, telling someone through the doorway what he wants in exact figures: "A loaf for each man, a round of cheese. Wrapped, if you would."

Orla is at the long board in the kitchen with her sleeves pushed up, pulling loaves out of the oven mouth on a peel. She doesn't stop for Vosk. "A loaf a man and a round of cheese. You'll have them by the time your mules are in." Then she sees Pell behind him with the bowl.

She takes it out of his hands, turns it over once and looks at the inside, which is scraped clean. "Well. You ate it cold." It doesn't sound like a complaint. The bowl goes onto a stack of others by the wash-tub. "That's your tab square, Master Dunmore. Pim gave me your silver last night, and the copper he kept, I'd guess." Her eyes go briefly to the common room. "Your mug's still on my mantel. Bring it on your way out and we're quits."

She sets the peel against the oven wall and wipes her hands down her apron. "Hot bread's a copper a heel if you want it now and not on the road. Small beer's two coppers the cup, and it's weak, before you ask." She jerks her chin at the ceiling. "Nobody's come down from upstairs but carters. Your shield man went up a few minutes back, and not toward the loft."

Behind her the loaves sit steaming on the board. Vosk waits in the passage with his ledger under his arm, watching the window over the wash-tub. It's getting lighter.

Nobody needs anything more from Pell before the mules go in, unless he wants something.

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) Vosk paid Pell, not the other way round: he counted four gold into Pell's hand last round, and Pell put it in his purse this turn. Handing the bowl back to Orla couldn't go wrong, so it needed no roll, and the story shows it done.

2) Pell went in to *see* whether there was hot bread and weak ale. He didn't say he was buying any, so Orla gave him the prices (a copper a heel, two coppers a cup) and his player decides whether he buys. She did answer the question about Sefa: nobody has come down but carters, and Bram went up a few minutes ago.
````

## Round 22

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Sefa Marrin (order: Bram, Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Pell Dunmore is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram takes the hand. His is big and rough and cold from the yard, and he doesn't crush hers; he shakes it once, the way he'd shake a hand over a finished deal.

"Staying's a fair answer. Better than walking a road you don't believe in." He lets go. "And I'll keep the condition. Someone wakes cold, we stop. He'll argue — he's told me so twice — and I'll let him, and then we'll stop anyway. I've been bargained an extra mile before. It's a mile too far every time."

He glances once at the straw on his own mail, as she did, and brushes at it without much effect.

"High Hedge. If the old man opens his gate to you, ask him about the circle with three strokes. The one someone scraped off before Nashkel. Vosk doesn't know it, Harn couldn't read it, and you didn't know it either, which makes it the only thing on that box nobody's got a story for." A short pause. "I'm the last man to tell a healer her business. But if it was mine I'd want that answered before the wax."

He steps down a tread to give her the landing.

"Safe road to you too, Mistress Marrin. The Elfsong, if I'm in the Gate and standing — I'll be the one with his back to the wall." Then he goes down, the third and seventh steps creaking under him, and out through the yard door into the grey, to the privy, the trough, the loaf and cheese, and the lead mule's head, shield on his arm and eyes on the gate.

Sefa Marrin: “My mind’s made up, Bram. I’m staying in Beregost today.”

I leave my hand offered for the farewell. “Good luck to you.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Bram takes her hand. His palm is broad and rough and still cold from the yard. He doesn't crush it. He shakes once, the way a man shakes on a finished deal, and lets go.

"Staying's a fair answer. Better than walking a road you don't believe in. And I'll keep the condition. Someone wakes cold, we stop. He'll argue, he's told me so twice, and I'll let him, and then we'll stop anyway. I've been bargained an extra mile before. It's a mile too far every time."

He looks down at the straw caught in his mail, the way she did a moment ago, and brushes at it. Most of it stays where it is.

"High Hedge. If the old man opens his gate to you, ask him about the circle with three strokes. The one someone scraped off before Nashkel. Vosk doesn't know it, Harn couldn't read it, and you didn't know it either. That makes it the only thing on that box nobody's got a story for." He pauses. "I'm the last man to tell a healer her business. But if it was mine, I'd want that answered before the wax."

He steps down a tread to leave her the landing.

"My mind's made up, Bram. I'm staying in Beregost today." Her hand is still half-raised from the shake. "Good luck to you."

He nods to it as though she'd handed him something to carry. "Safe road to you too, Mistress Marrin. The Elfsong, if I'm in the Gate and still standing. I'll be the one with his back to the wall."

Then he goes down. The seventh step creaks under him, then the third, and the bulk of shield and mail turns at the foot of the stairs toward the yard door and is gone.

---

Sefa is alone on the landing. Grey light from the small window lies across the boards. Behind her, the door of her room stands open on the unmade bed and the chipped jug. Down in the common room someone drags a bench across the floor. A carter in the loft finally finds his other boot and swears at it with deep relief. Bread smells come up the stairwell, and woodsmoke, and through the wall on the yard side she hears the faint knock of harness and a mule blowing.

She has the day to herself. High Hedge is an hour west by the road Pell came in on, and Orla said its gate is open by daylight. The temple is somewhere in the town. And the room is paid until she leaves it. Nothing calls her anywhere else unless she decides to go.

---

The yard has turned from slate to pale. Bram does his rounds the way he said he would. He uses the privy. At the trough the water breaks a thin skin of cold, and he takes two handfuls of it over his face and the back of his neck, which brings him the rest of the way awake. He walks along the row past the first wagon to where the lead team will stand, takes the shield off his back and settles it on his arm.

Pim already has both of the middle team out by the trough in harness. He's leading the first lead mule out of the stable when Bram reaches the wagon. The animal sees Bram's shield and pulls its head back once. Then it decides he's furniture.

The yard door opens and Vosk comes out with a bundle in oiled cloth under one arm and the ledger under the other. He looks at the eastern sky over the stable roof, then down at the page. He doesn't need a lamp to read it any more. He walks to the first wagon, loosens the tail lashing and lays the bundle inside beside the spoiled linen bolt, which has already been moved there. Then he ties it off again.

"Three loaves and a round of cheese, Master Halloway. Mistress Tarnwick's, not hardtack. As agreed." He turns to the stable. "Pim. Into the shafts. The middle team first."

"The middle team first. Into the shafts." Pim backs the first mule toward the middle wagon's tongue. The hum is coming up faint through those boards, the same as all night, and the mule goes in between the shafts without a toss of its head. It stands and lets the boy buckle it.

Vosk looks along the yard once: the barred gate, the logs still leaning at the fence, the yard door. Then he looks at Bram at the head of the lead team.

"Master Dunmore is inside still. When he comes out, we are three." He doesn't say *and the fourth stayed*, but his eyes go briefly to the upper window.

*(Out of game: Bram had already shaken Sefa's hand by the time she spoke, so I played her words as her goodbye while he was stepping down.)*
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 22 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell pulls three coppers from his purse and sets them on the board's edge. "Hot bread and a cup. Weak's fine." He takes the heel Orla passes him, wraps it mostly in his fingers rather than his palm, and stands just clear of the kitchen doorway where the heat pours past him and he isn't in anyone's road.

He eats the bread slowly, blowing over each bite more from habit than need. Between mouthfuls he tracks the sounds of the house: the clink behind him in the kitchen, the scrape and thump of men dressing and coming down the stairs, any sign of Sefa's voice. In the yard, Pim's low steady cursing at a harness strap carries through with the first scrape of the yoking.

He finishes the bread, knuckles the crumbs off his hands, and takes his weak ale by the cup. Then he moves back toward the low end of the common room, where the carter still sleeps, and stands where he can watch both the stair and the front door.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Orla scoops the three coppers off the edge of the board without counting them. She breaks a heel off the end of the nearest loaf, steam and all, and holds it out on her flat palm. Then she draws a cup of small beer from the cask under the window and sets it beside his hand. "One and two. Square." She has already turned back to the oven.

The heel is too hot to hold properly, so Pell holds it in his fingertips. He stands just clear of the doorway, where the oven's heat rolls out past him into the cold passage and he isn't in anyone's way. The crust cracks when he bites it. The inside is soft and grey-white, and it tastes of the oven more than of salt. The beer is weak, as she said, thin and a little sour, and cold from the cask.

The house goes on around him. Behind him a pot lid rings on the board and Orla tells the fire something short. Upstairs, boots go across the loft, a door bangs and a carter laughs at somebody. Out in the yard, through two doors and a passage, Pim is talking to a harness strap in a low, steady mutter that never quite becomes words. There's the knock of a tongue being lifted, and a mule blows.

Halfway through the heel, the stairs speak. It's a heavy tread. The seventh step creaks, then the third, and mail chinks with each step. Bram comes off the bottom stair with straw still on his shoulders and the shield slung on his back. He doesn't look round the common room. He turns at the foot of the stairs toward the yard door and goes out through it, and the cold comes in around the door for a moment before it shuts.

Nobody comes down after him. Up at the top of the stairs it's quiet. It isn't a sleeping quiet. Someone is standing up there, but they say nothing Pell can hear.

He finishes the bread and knocks the crumbs off his knuckles. He carries the cup down to the low end of the common room, past the hearth. The new flames are taking the wood properly now. On the mantel above it, his empty cider mug from last night is still standing where he left it. The carter is still asleep sitting up at the end bench, his cheek on his folded arms and his breath whistling. From where Pell stands now, he can see the foot of the stairs and the front door onto the Coast Way. The door is still barred. The grey through its cracks has turned the colour of milk.

Out in the yard Vosk is waiting for him. Nothing else calls Pell before then unless he wants it to.
````


## Round 23

### Scene: Bram Halloway (order: Bram)

*Bram Halloway pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram runs a hand down the lead mule's neck, finds the harness buckle at the collar and gives it a tug to be sure Pim's drawn it tight, then checks the trace on the near side. Old habit. He'd do it on a run he trusted and he'll do it twice on this one.

"The fourth read your wax for you and asked nothing for it," he says to Vosk, not looking up from the buckle. "That's more than Harn did for his two days. Don't count her against you." He straightens. "And when Dunmore comes out, he walks the middle wagon's off side where I can see him from the lead, and you sit the middle bench where you always do. If I call halt, the lead team stops first and the others close up. Nobody passes the lead. That's the whole of the drill, and it's the same one that's kept me alive ten years."

He settles the shield, looks once more at the fence where the logs lean, then at the gate.

"When he's out and he's said his yes to your face, Pim lifts the bar and we go. I'd like to be past the north road-post before the sergeant's had his breakfast. Hungry men are curious men."
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The buckle at the lead mule's collar holds when Bram tugs it, and so does the near-side trace. Pim drew both tight, and he did it right, with the tongue of the strap tucked back under its keeper. The mule turns its head round at Bram's hand and blows warm breath against his mail, then loses interest.

Vosk stands by the first wagon's tail with the ledger under his arm and hears Bram out. At *the fourth* his eyes go up to the window again.

"I don't count her against me, Master Halloway. She looked at the crate when nobody asked her to, and she told me the truth about what she couldn't see. I would rather have that from her than a yes she didn't mean." Then the drill, and he takes it the same way he took the terms last night, point by point. "The middle bench, as always. You call the halt, and the lead stops first. Nobody passes the lead. Agreed."

He shifts the ledger.

"On Master Dunmore, though, you are a quarter hour behind. He gave me his yes in this yard while you were on the stairs. Plain terms, the same as yours, and he took his four." He glances at the yard door. "He went in with Mistress Tarnwick's bowl. When he comes out, we are ready."

At *the sergeant's breakfast* the corner of his mouth tightens. "The north post is a corporal at this hour, and in my experience he is curious about everything until he has seen a stamp and a few silver. I'll do the talking there, if you don't object. It goes quicker when the man asking the questions is the man with the paper."

Behind them Pim comes out of the stable leading the second lead mule. He backs it up beside its partner, talking to the harness under his breath, and starts on the collar buckle without being told. The middle team stands quiet in the shafts. A faint hum comes up through the middle wagon's boards into the tongue and the traces, and neither mule pays it any mind.

The light over the stable roof has gone from pale grey to plain morning. The logs still lean against the fence, and the bar is still down across the gate. All they're waiting on now is the yard door.
````

````text
The light over the stable roof has gone from pale grey to plain morning. The logs still lean against the fence, and the bar is still down across the gate. All you're waiting on now is the yard door.
````

Together after: [Bram Halloway]

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I go downstairs and find Orla.

“Good morning. What do I owe for bread, cheese and something hot? And which turning takes me onto the High Hedge road?”

Over breakfast, I try to fix the scraped sigil in my memory, keeping what I actually saw separate from what the missing strokes might have been. I won’t improve it by guessing.

Once I’ve eaten and paid, I set out west in daylight. I intend to approach High Hedge openly, by the road, and ask whether Thalantyr receives visitors seeking advice on a magical mark—and what he charges for the consultation.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa comes down the stairs with her pack on her shoulder. The third step creaks under her, then the seventh. The common room is warmer than the landing. The hearth has caught properly, the benches are mostly empty, and a carter sleeps sitting up at the end bench with his cheek on his arms. At the low end of the room, near the fire, Pell Dunmore stands with a cup in his hand.

Orla is in the kitchen with her sleeves pushed up, knocking loaves out of their tins onto the board. She looks up when Sefa reaches the doorway.

"Good morning. What do I owe for bread, cheese and something hot? And which turning takes me onto the High Hedge road?"

"Hot bread's a copper a heel. Cheese is two the wedge. Hot is cider, five, the same pot as last night, and it's better for sitting." Orla breaks a heel off the end loaf and puts it on a board, cuts a wedge from a round under a cloth and sets it beside the bread. She ladles cider from the pot at the hearth's edge into a clay mug. "Eight coppers. Sit where you like, there's room now."

She wipes her knife on her apron and points it at the front of the house.

"Out the front, turn right, down the Way toward the temple end. Past the last smithy there's a horse trough where the road splits. The left fork goes west. That's your road. Stay on it and you'll see the hedge before you see the house. It's that kind of hedge." She has turned back to her tins already. "An hour, if you don't stop to look at things."

Sefa sits at the near end of the long table. The bread is too hot to tear cleanly. The cheese is hard and sharp, and the cider tastes of clove, warm through the clay. Between mouthfuls she goes over the mark the way she would go over a dressing she had to describe to the sister on the next shift: only what was there. A burned circle on the end board, low down. Three short strokes cutting into it that don't meet in the middle. More than half of it scraped away, the scrape lines grey and old. She doesn't know how many strokes there were before the blade went over them, or where they ran. She leaves that part blank.

She counts eight coppers onto the board by the kitchen door. Orla sweeps them into her apron without turning round. "Square."

---

The front door opens onto the Coast Way in plain morning light. The street smells of fresh coal smoke. Down the road the first forges are opening their shutters, and a smith's boy is throwing ash into the gutter by the bucketful. A cart goes north loaded with barrel staves. Sefa turns right, toward the temple end, as Orla said. She walks past the cooper's shop and four smithies with their doors open and the hammers just starting. At the last one the road widens around a stone horse trough with a skin of cold on the water, and splits.

The left fork runs west out of town between low stone walls, and Beregost falls behind her quickly. The hammering thins until it sounds like a clock in another room, then it's gone. There are fields on both sides, stubble and dark ploughed earth with frost still white in the furrows on the shaded side. A crow walks in one of them and ignores her. Half an hour out, a farmer with a handcart full of turnips comes the other way. He nods to her and says the morning's a cold one, which it is, and goes on toward town. After that she has the road to herself for a long stretch. Her boots are loud on the frozen ruts. The sun comes up behind her and throws her shadow out ahead on the road, long and thin.

Orla was right about the hedge. Sefa sees it a good while before anything else: a dark green wall across the land ahead to the left of the road, too straight and too tall for any farmer's boundary. As she gets closer she can see it's yew, clipped flat on top, easily twice a man's height. It runs a long way in both directions. Over it shows the top of a building, a narrow tower of grey stone with one window facing the road and a thread of smoke going straight up from a chimney in the still air.

A short track leaves the road and runs up to a gap in the yew. In the gap stands a gate of black iron bars, shut. Through the bars she can see a gravel path and more hedge beyond, so she can't see the house from here. Beside the gate an iron bell hangs from a bracket with a pull-chain under it. A small board is nailed to the gatepost, its paint worn but legible:

*CALLERS BY DAYLIGHT. RING ONCE.*

Nobody is at the gate. Somewhere past the hedge a bird calls once and stops. The smoke from the chimney goes on rising. It's a little past a quarter to eight.

*(Out of game: Pell was in the common room when Sefa came down. Anything that passed between them belongs to his player and his scene, so I've only shown where he was standing.)*

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) No roll was needed: buying breakfast at the inn couldn't go wrong. Sefa paid Orla eight coppers and ate.

2) No roll was needed, since she was just asking the way. Orla told her: right down the Way, past the last smithy, then the left fork west at the horse trough.

3) No roll was needed, because Sefa saw the mark herself last night and only remembered what was there. The strokes that were scraped away stay unknown, as she wanted.

4) No roll was needed: walking an hour down an open road in daylight is just travel. She's at High Hedge's gate now, and she hasn't rung the bell yet.
````

### Scene: Pell Dunmore (order: Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 23 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Sefa Marrin are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Pell Dunmore: Pell sets the empty cup on the mantel beside the cider mug from the night before, two clay vessels side by side like bookends to the watch.

Then he glances once toward the top of the stairs. The silence up there is the sort that means someone is listening back. He doesn't call out. He lets his heels fall on the floor as he crosses to the yard door, just enough noise that whoever is standing in the dark upstairs knows he's gone and can make her own choice without an audience.

He eases the door open and steps through. Cold air and the smell of mules and cold iron meet him. He pulls the door to behind him without a bang, then walks toward the middle wagon to see where Bram wants him.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Pell sets the empty cup on the mantel beside last night's cider mug. Two clay pots stand side by side above the new fire. The carter at the end bench whistles on in his sleep.

Pell looks once at the top of the stairs. The quiet up there hasn't changed. He doesn't call out. He crosses the floor letting his heels come down, loud enough for anyone on the landing to hear a man leaving, and puts his hand on the latch of the yard door. Behind him the third stair creaks once under a light step. Then he's through the door and pulling it to, and it shuts without a bang.

The yard is cold and smells of mules and wet straw. Above the stable roof it's plain morning now, and the frost has gone grey and soft on the shaded side of the trough. The middle team stands in the shafts. The faint hum comes up through the wagon's tongue and traces, and neither mule pays it any mind. At the far end of the row Pim is on his toes at the second lead mule's collar. He pulls the buckle through, tucks the strap's tongue under its keeper the way Bram checked the first one, and steps back, wiping his hands on his shirt. Bram stands at the lead team's head with the shield on his arm, his eyes on the gate.

Vosk is at the tail of the first wagon with the ledger under his arm. He sees Pell come out and heads him off before he reaches the middle wagon. He keeps his voice low, though there's nobody in the yard who doesn't already know the business.

"Master Dunmore. Master Halloway has set your place while you were inside. The middle wagon's off side, the far side from the stable, where he can see you from the lead. If he calls a halt, the lead stops first and the others close up. Nobody passes the lead." He goes through it the way he'd read a clause back to a buyer. "That is the drill. It's his, not mine."

He looks along the row. The lead team is harnessed, the middle team is in the shafts, and Orla's wrapped bread is in the first wagon's tail.

"We are three, and ready." His eyes go to the man at the lead mule's head and stay there. "The bar waits on his word."

Pim has stopped by the gate with one hand on the bar's end, his weight already on his back foot. He's watching Bram too.
````

