## Round 24

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

*Bram Halloway pressed [Onward] with text with no card of theirs waiting — the text kept as the message.*

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram grunts at that — the nearest he comes to being caught out.

"Then he's quicker to a yes than he is to a question. Fair enough. Four days to find out which serves him better." He checks the second mule's collar after Pim out of the same habit, finds it tight, and gives the boy a nod that costs nothing and is worth something.

"The post is yours, Master Vosk. You've the paper and the Amnian. I'll stand at the lead team's head and look like a man who's done this before, which is the only thing a corporal wants from a guard. If he asks me anything, it'll be the weather, and I'll tell him it's holding." He glances at the sky to be sure he's not lying. "Dunmore sees the look you've seen off the fence. If the corporal gets curious about the middle wagon, he'll have two men between him and it before he's finished the thought. Not hands on him. Just in the way."

He settles the shield, rests his sword hand on the pommel, and watches the yard door for Pell.

"When he's out, lift the bar. Pim, you did the harness right. Keep doing it that way and someone'll pay you for it one day."

Pell Dunmore: Pell nods once and keeps his voice pitched for the yard, not the street beyond. "Off side of the middle wagon, in Bram's sight. Stop when the lead stops. Pass no one." He repeats it the way he'd repeat a contract clause, without impatience, so the factor can hear that it's set.

He glances once down the row to where Bram stands at the lead team's head, then to Pim at the gate. "I saw the upstairs light this morning, but not the woman. If she's not down by now, she's not in." He gives it to Vosk as a simple fact, with no question in it. "It's your run and his line. I walk where I'm put."

Then he steps clear of the first wagon's shadow and makes his way to the middle wagon's far side, the side away from the stable, his feet finding the drier ground by old habit. He chooses a spot a stride back from the rearmost wheel, just ahead of the tailboard and in clear view of the lead team, and lets his cloak settle. One hand rests easy on the strap of his satchel, close to a healing potion if it comes to that; the other hangs loose. His head is up, and his eyes do their slow work: the gate, the tethered mules, the eastern light over the rooftops, the lane beyond the fence.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
"Then he's quicker to a yes than he is to a question." Bram grunts. "Fair enough. Four days to find out which serves him better."

He goes down to the second lead mule and checks its collar after Pim, out of the same habit as before. The buckle is tight and the strap's tongue is tucked under its keeper. Bram gives the boy a nod. Pim goes red to the ears and looks at the mule instead of at him.

Vosk hears the rest out with the ledger shut under his arm. The post is his, since he has the paper and the Amnian. Bram will stand at the lead team's head looking like a man who has done this before, and if the corporal asks him anything it will be about the weather, which is holding. Vosk follows Bram's glance up at the sky. It is pale and clear over the stable roof, with no cloud anywhere.

"Then we are agreed, Master Halloway. Not hands on him. Only in the way." He says it like a clause he has read back and found sound.

Pell comes down the row and stops by the first wagon. He says the drill back at yard pitch: off side of the middle wagon, in Bram's sight, stop when the lead stops, pass no one. Then he looks at Vosk. "I saw the upstairs light this morning, but not the woman. If she's not down by now, she's not in. It's your run and his line. I walk where I'm put."

"Mistress Marrin is staying in Beregost, Master Dunmore. She told Master Halloway so on the stairs, and she told me last night." Vosk doesn't look at the upper window this time. "So we are three, as I said."

Pell goes round the middle wagon to its far side, away from the stable. He picks his way along the drier ground and stops a stride back from the rear wheel, just ahead of the tailboard, where Bram at the lead can see him down the length of the row. His cloak settles. One hand rests on the strap of the satchel.

*When he's out, lift the bar.* He is out.

"Lift the bar, master. Yes." Pim gets his shoulder under the bar and lays it on the ground without a bang. He drags both leaves of the gate inward, one after the other, and their bottom edges scrape over the packed earth. The Coast Way lies past them, grey-gold in the early light, empty except for a smith's boy with an ash bucket and a dog nosing the gutter. On his way back past the lead team Pim mutters to himself, "Keep doing it that way," and climbs onto the first wagon's bench as if that's where he belongs. He's driving it to the post and walking back. Nobody told him to. Vosk lets it stand.

Vosk climbs onto the middle bench, the one in front of the crate, and gathers the reins. He looks at Bram.

The lead team leans into the collars and the first wagon rolls out under the gate beam onto the road. The middle team goes after it without a toss of the head. A faint hum comes up through the shafts, the same as all night, and the near mule flicks one ear at a fly and nothing more. The third wagon creaks out last. Behind them the yard is empty except for six logs leaning against the fence.

---

They turn north up the Coast Way. Beregost is waking on both sides of the road. Shutters bang open, a forge door rolls back on a bed of coals, and a woman throws a basin of water into the street, then looks hard at the mules as if they'd done it. They pass the cooper's shop with its step empty and go on up the street to the Red Sheaf, which is shuttered tight. Last night's straw has been swept into a heap against its wall and the door is shut. There's nobody on its step and no red cloth anywhere along the street.

The houses thin out past the last smithies. Where the town ends the road narrows between two low stone walls, and across it stands the north post: a lean-to of weathered boards, a pole barrier on a pivot, and a brazier of last night's coals with two men warming their hands over it. They wear the green tabards of the Beregost militia. One is grey and heavy and asleep on his feet. The other is younger, with a corporal's knot on his shoulder and a sergeant's hopes in his face, and he steps out into the road with one hand up before the lead mule's nose has reached the pole.

Vosk climbs down from the middle bench before he's asked. He walks forward with the paper already out, a folded sheet with a stamp in dark wax at its foot, and holds it so the corporal can see the stamp without taking the sheet.

"Gerrit Vosk, cloth factor, out of Nashkel for Baldur's Gate. Three wagons, wool and linen, and two guards. That is my bill for the cargo."

The corporal looks at the stamp for a long moment, the way a man looks at writing he can't read and doesn't want to say so. "Amnian."

"Amnian," Vosk agrees. Three silver pieces have found their way into the hand under the paper. "And this for the road, corporal, with my thanks for the early hour."

The silver goes where silver goes. The corporal walks down the first wagon, as a man does who has been paid and wants it to look like work, and twitches up the canvas at its tail. He finds Orla's oiled bundle, and beside it the spoiled linen bolt, still dark along one edge. He puts two fingers on it.

"Wet."

"Spoiled on the road. It lay against a sweating board," Vosk says, and that's true as far as it goes. "You're welcome to it for a horse-blanket, if you've a use for one."

The corporal snorts and lets the canvas drop. He doesn't go on to the middle wagon. He stops level with the lead team instead and looks Bram up and down: the mended shoulder, the painted-over shield, and the dust of four days ground into his boots that one wash at a trough hasn't touched.

"You came up from Nashkel with this lot?" He jerks his chin south, back down the Way. "Or on your own?"

 *(Out of game, one line each:)*

1) There was no roll, because standing at the lead team's head and watching the yard door couldn't go wrong. Bram stood there until Pell came round the middle wagon, and then the bar came up on his word.

2) There was no roll for this either, since it's just taking up a position. Pell stood a stride back from the middle wagon's rear wheel on the off side, in Bram's sight, and kept that place all the way to the north post.

3) That one wasn't a player's action. Vosk is a person of the world, and he climbed onto the middle bench and took up the reins himself as part of rolling out. Nothing needed a roll.
````

````text
You go down to the second lead mule and check its collar after Pim, out of the same habit as before. The buckle is tight and the strap's tongue is tucked under its keeper. You give the boy a nod. Pim goes red to the ears and looks at the mule instead of at you.

"Spoiled on the road. It lay against a sweating board," Vosk says. "You're welcome to it for a horse-blanket, if you've a use for one."

The corporal snorts and lets the canvas drop. He doesn't go on to the middle wagon. He stops level with the lead team instead and looks you up and down: the mended shoulder, the painted-over shield, and the dust of four days ground into your boots that one wash at a trough hasn't touched.

"You came up from Nashkel with this lot?" He jerks his chin south, back down the Way. "Or on your own?"
````

**Pell Dunmore's screen (the relay):**

````text
When you come out of the yard door, the big guard, Bram, is standing at the lead team's head with his shield settled and his sword hand resting on the pommel, watching the door. Vosk stands by with the ledger shut under his arm. Pim waits at the gate.

"Mistress Marrin is staying in Beregost, Master Dunmore. She told Master Halloway so on the stairs, and she told me last night." Vosk doesn't look at the upper window this time. "So we are three, as I said."

You go round the middle wagon to its far side, away from the stable, and stop a stride back from the rear wheel, just ahead of the tailboard, where Bram at the lead can see you down the length of the row.

You are out, and the gate opens.

"Lift the bar, master. Yes." Pim gets his shoulder under the bar and lays it on the ground without a bang. He drags both leaves of the gate inward, one after the other, and their bottom edges scrape over the packed earth. The Coast Way lies past them, grey-gold in the early light, empty except for a smith's boy with an ash bucket and a dog nosing the gutter. On his way back past the lead team Pim mutters something to himself and climbs onto the first wagon's bench as if that's where he belongs. He's driving it to the post and walking back. Nobody told him to. Vosk lets it stand.

Vosk climbs onto the middle bench, the one in front of the crate, and gathers the reins. He looks at Bram.

The lead team leans into the collars and the first wagon rolls out under the gate beam onto the road. The middle team goes after it without a toss of the head. A faint hum comes up through the shafts, the same as all night, and the near mule flicks one ear at a fly and nothing more. The third wagon creaks out last. Behind you the yard is empty except for six logs leaning against the fence.

---

You turn north up the Coast Way. Beregost is waking on both sides of the road. Shutters bang open, a forge door rolls back on a bed of coals, and a woman throws a basin of water into the street, then looks hard at the mules as if they'd done it. You pass the cooper's shop with its step empty and go on up the street to the Red Sheaf, which is shuttered tight. Last night's straw has been swept into a heap against its wall and the door is shut. There's nobody on its step and no red cloth anywhere along the street.

The houses thin out past the last smithies. Where the town ends the road narrows between two low stone walls, and across it stands the north post: a lean-to of weathered boards, a pole barrier on a pivot, and a brazier of last night's coals with two men warming their hands over it. They wear the green tabards of the Beregost militia. One is grey and heavy and asleep on his feet. The other is younger, with a corporal's knot on his shoulder and a sergeant's hopes in his face, and he steps out into the road with one hand up before the lead mule's nose has reached the pole.

Vosk climbs down from the middle bench before he's asked. He walks forward with the paper already out, a folded sheet with a stamp in dark wax at its foot, and holds it so the corporal can see the stamp without taking the sheet.

"Gerrit Vosk, cloth factor, out of Nashkel for Baldur's Gate. Three wagons, wool and linen, and two guards. That is my bill for the cargo."

The corporal looks at the stamp for a long moment, the way a man looks at writing he can't read and doesn't want to say so. "Amnian."

"Amnian," Vosk agrees. Three silver pieces have found their way into the hand under the paper. "And this for the road, corporal, with my thanks for the early hour."

The silver goes where silver goes. The corporal walks down the first wagon, as a man does who has been paid and wants it to look like work, and twitches up the canvas at its tail. He finds Orla's oiled bundle, and beside it the spoiled linen bolt, still dark along one edge. He puts two fingers on it.

"Wet."

"Spoiled on the road. It lay against a sweating board," Vosk says. "You're welcome to it for a horse-blanket, if you've a use for one."

The corporal snorts and lets the canvas drop. He doesn't go on to the middle wagon. He stops level with the lead team instead and looks Bram up and down: the mended shoulder, the painted-over shield, and the dust of four days ground into his boots that one wash at a trough hasn't touched.

"You came up from Nashkel with this lot?" He jerks his chin south, back down the Way. "Or on your own?"
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 24 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: I pull the bell-chain once, then step back so I’m plainly visible from the gate, with my hands empty.

If someone answers, I say, “Good morning. Sefa Marrin. I’m seeking Master Thalantyr’s advice about a damaged magical mark I saw on a sealed crate. I haven’t brought the object, only a description. Is he receiving callers, and what would a consultation cost?”

I wait without ringing again or trying the gate.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa pulls the chain once. The bell is black iron like the gate and has no clapper she can see, but it gives one low, round note that carries over the yew. A moment later the same note comes back from the tower, faint, as if a second bell up there had been struck from the inside. Then both stop. Sefa steps back from the bars with her hands empty and waits.

For a while there's only the smoke going straight up from the chimney and a blackbird working in the frost under the hedge. Then gravel crunches beyond the inner hedge. The steps are slow and even, the steps of a man who doesn't see why he should hurry.

He comes round the corner of the yew: an old man, tall and very thin, in a heavy grey robe with a sheepskin jerkin pulled on over it against the cold. His beard is white and cut short, square across the bottom. He's carrying a clay mug that steams. His left hand has ink on two fingers, old ink worked into the creases, and there's a pair of wire-rimmed lenses pushed up onto his forehead. He stops a pace short of the gate, doesn't open it, and looks at her through the bars the way a man reads the first line of a letter to decide whether to read the rest.

She tells him her name and her business: a damaged mark on a sealed crate, a description and not the object, and she asks whether he's receiving callers and what a consultation costs.

He drinks from the mug before he answers.

"I am receiving you, Mistress Marrin. That's not the same as receiving callers." His voice is dry and exact, with a faint southern edge on some of the vowels, the kind that has worn down over a long time. "You've brought nothing, which is either very sensible or very convenient. I'll find out which."

He lowers the lenses onto his nose and studies her for a moment: the beads at her throat, the pale book at her belt. His eyes stay on the book a breath longer than on anything else, then move back to her face.

"Five gold. For that you get my attention until this mug is cold, and my honest opinion at the end. If I know the mark, you get its name and whatever goes with the name. If I don't, you get my reasons for not knowing. Those are worth less, and you pay the same." He tips the mug a little toward her. "I don't take coin through a gate, and I don't open the gate to someone who hasn't decided. And I'll want to know whose crate, and where it is now. Not to go after it. A mark means one thing on a pedlar's box and another on a Flaming Fist strongbox, and I don't give opinions blind."

He waits, with the steam rising between them and the bar of the gate still in its socket.

"Well? Are you coming in, or did you walk an hour to look at my hedge?"
````

## Round 25

### Scene: Bram Halloway, Pell Dunmore (order: Bram, Pell)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 25 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Sefa Marrin is elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate that character here.)

Bram Halloway: Bram doesn't shift the shield and doesn't look at Vosk. A guard who checks with his factor before answering a corporal is a guard with something to check.

"On my own. Four days up from Nashkel, paid off there, hired on here last night when he was two men short." He says it the way he'd give his name at a tollgate — a fact, not a story. "Road south of here's empty, corporal, if that's worth anything to you. One tinker and one of your patrols the whole way, and the ruts were old. Nobody's running wagons that stretch just now."

He nods at the sky, since he said he would. "Weather's holding. We'd like to make the most of it."

Then he waits, hand resting on the pommel, eyes on the pole barrier and not on the man — the look of someone who's stood at a hundred of these and expects this one to lift like the rest.

Pell Dunmore: Pell stays where he is on the off side, his eyes drifting past the corporal toward the road ahead and the ditches beyond the post. He doesn't set his hand on his sword; his posture is that of a hired man paid to stand and look about him, and he lets the inspection pass through him like weather. But he's listening, nails in a hinge, and he watches the younger militiaman's knuckles on the pole even while Vosk's silver disappears.

When the corporal stops to study Bram, Pell shifts his weight just enough to bring the length of the middle wagon into his view, and his eyes come back along its near flank. The canvas is tight. The mule hasn't turned its head. Still, he counts the paces from the grey, heavy guardsman to the pole, and the open ground on either side. A man on his side of the road would have to cross the mules. A man behind them, among the last carts and the waking town, would have to go through Pell. He seems in no hurry, and not afraid of boredom.
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
The corporal hears Bram out with his thumbs hooked in his belt. At *the ruts were old* his face changes. He looks less like a man who wants to be sergeant and more like a man adding something up.

"Old ruts." He glances down the road, back the way they came, as if he could see the Nashkel stretch from here. "You're the third this tenday who's told me that. A drover on Fourthday, and a priest on a mule before him." He chews the inside of his cheek. "There's two trains out of Nashkel that should have been through this post by now, and they haven't been. Merchants come up here asking me where their cloth is. Like I'd know." He stops there. It's more than he meant to say to a hired man, and he knows it.

He looks Bram up and down once more: the boots, the mended shoulder, the shield. Then he steps back from the lead mule's nose.

"One tinker and a patrol. I'll tell the captain." He jerks his chin at the grey man by the brazier. "Hobb. The pole."

The grey man takes his hands off the coals with a sigh. He leans his weight on the pole's short end, and the long end swings up out of the road on its pivot, creaking.

Pell stands on the off side of the middle wagon through all of it and doesn't do anything a hired man wouldn't. He sees what there is to see. The corporal's hands stay at his belt the whole time, and the grey man never touches his spear, which leans against the lean-to with frost still on the shaft. The ditches on either side of the post are shallow and empty, with white grass in them. Behind them the Coast Way runs back into Beregost: a cart of barrel staves far down the street, smoke from a dozen chimneys and nobody on foot. The corporal never looks at the middle wagon at all. When the pole goes up, the near mule in the middle team flicks an ear at the creak and nothing more.

Vosk folds the paper and puts it inside his coat. "Thank you, corporal." He walks back down the row and stops beside the first wagon, where Pim is already climbing down off the bench with the reins gathered in one hand.

"Here's me, master." Pim holds the reins up toward whoever will take them. "Walk back now. Mistress Tarnwick said straight back."

Vosk takes his ledger from under his arm and looks at Bram.

"I have no carter past this post, Master Halloway. I hadn't counted on Pim for more than the town. The third team follows on its line from my tail, the way it came out of the yard. But someone has to drive the first, or walk the lead team's heads all day." He doesn't push it. "Which would you rather?"

Beyond the pole the Coast Way runs north between the two low walls and then out into open country. It's pale gold in the early light, empty, with frost on the verges.
````

### Scene: Sefa Marrin (order: Sefa)

**What the DM was sent:**

````text
(Round 25 — one paragraph per character in this scene. Bram Halloway and Pell Dunmore are elsewhere this round, in a scene of their own that comes to you as its own message: do not narrate them here.)

Sefa Marrin: “I’m coming in. Five gold for an honest opinion is agreed.”

I reach for my purse without approaching the bars.

“The carrier is Gerrit Vosk, a cloth factor. The crate came from Nashkel on commission for a buyer whose name he wouldn’t give me. It was in the Burning Wizard’s yard last night, among three wagons of wool and linen. He intended to leave for Baldur’s Gate at first light. I haven’t witnessed his departure.”

I meet his eyes. “I promised discretion about the seal while he remained in Beregost. The damaged mark is separate from it. I can describe that, the hum, and the unnatural cold without breaking my word. If you need more before you’ll accept the consultation, I’d rather settle that before either of us takes the other’s coin or time.”
````

**The DM's narration:**

````text
Sefa's hand finds the purse at her belt and stays there. She doesn't come any closer to the bars.

Thalantyr listens with the mug held under his chin, its steam drifting past the wire lenses. His face doesn't change at *a cloth factor*. At *Nashkel* one white eyebrow lifts a little, and at *I promised discretion about the seal* he lowers the mug.

He drinks before he answers.

"Gerrit Vosk. Cloth, Nashkel to the Gate, and a buyer with no name." He says it back flatly, like a clerk reading a docket. "If he meant first light, he's been an hour on the Coast Way already, so I needn't worry about him knocking on my gate next. That's context enough." He looks at her over the rim. "Keep your word about the seal. It's a sealed box out of Nashkel that a cloth factor won't open and a woman with prayer beads won't talk about. I can guess the colour of the wax without you."

He sets the mug on the flat top of the gatepost. Then he reaches through the bars to the inside bracket, lifts the iron bar out with both hands and leans it against the yew. One leaf of the gate swings inward on hinges that someone keeps oiled.

"In, then. You've decided."

Sefa steps through. The gate closes behind her, and he drops the bar back into its brackets with a short iron knock. Then he holds out his left hand, palm up, with the ink worked into the creases of two fingers.

"Five."

Sefa counts five gold pieces out of her purse into his palm. He goes over them once with his thumb, exactly as he said he would, and they go into the sheepskin jerkin. "Paid." He picks the mug up again. "And the mug's running."

The gravel path runs between two walls of yew clipped as square as the outer hedge, so for a while she walks down a green corridor with frost still white along its shaded side. It turns twice. Then it opens onto a square of garden: herb beds bedded down under straw, a well with a wooden cover, and on the far side the grey tower rising from the earth with its door shut. The sun has cleared the hedge on the eastern side. Along that wall the frost has melted dark on a long stone bench.

Thalantyr sits at one end of it and leaves her the other. As she sits down his eyes go once more to the pale book at her belt, then come away. He says nothing about it.

"It's a cold morning, Mistress Marrin. I'd describe briskly." He wraps both hands round the mug. "The mark first. Where it was on the crate, what made it, and what's left. Tell me only what you saw. If you start telling me what it might have been, I'll stop you."
````
